Measured with a bare listener and 48 non-blocking connects, 0.3 s before the first `accept()` (macOS, `somaxconn` 128): a backlog of 8 gave 8 connections, 16 gave 16, 32 gave 32 and 64 gave all 48; with 8 the other 40 had not come after 12.5 s. Both listeners hold 64 now (`kListenBacklog`; the control interface's had 64 already): `test_tcp` N3.8 and `test_ws` W1.23.

**The frame loop: what a machine can do about its own part.** Every frame polls the network, runs the ticks that are due and then draws, in that order (`Application::run_frame_with_delta`: `handle_events`, `pump_network`, the simulation,
`render_frame`), so a turn that has arrived is run by the frame that follows it, never one frame later, and drawn in that same frame. Running a tick between frames would change the state earlier than the screen can show it: the
picture only changes at the frame, so a tick that waits for its frame costs the player nothing he can see (it costs the readout up to a frame: the delay is measured to the tick). The rest of the 17 - 21 ms that a window at 60 frames a
second adds (measured above) is the input being read at the start of the next frame (a click waits 0 - 16.7 ms for it before it is sent, 8 on average) and the frame that polls the turn and runs its tick (0 - 16.7 ms after the turn arrived, 8 on average). The browser has the same order inside `requestAnimationFrame`
(display rate, 60 - 144 frames a second; a WebSocket message waits in the page's queue until the next frame polls it, and the ping readout takes that wait off: above) and a page that is drawn rarely (the built-in browser pane's hidden page: about once a second; Chrome's
hidden tab until v0.0.95: not at all, see "A hidden tab"; since v0.0.96 the server's messages run it) keeps its game in step with real time by the long-frame rule above.
What would cut the input wait is reading the pointer's events between frames (a native loop that sleeps in slices) or a WebSocket that is read by the page's own event and sent at once: not done, for 8 ms of 100 - 160.

**Recommendations, with their numbers.**

* **Keep the buffer rule as it is** (1 - 4 turns, 95th percentile, grow at once up to the number that bridges a late turn, shrink a turn per 10 s): gain 75 ms on a steady link (50 for the buffer, 25 for the seal), nothing lost on a jittery
  one (0 - 60 ms of jitter: no stall, delay 164 against 252 - 301), and on a link with lost packets the same stalls as before (a spike of 250 ms stops the game for 150 - 200 ms in both). The risk is a link whose lateness is
  between 50 and 150 ms in one turn in twenty: it gets two or three turns (100 - 150 ms) until it calms down for 10 s per turn; a steady 50 ms jitter costs 50 ms more than a clean link, not more.
* **Do not go below 50 ms per turn**: a turn is a tick (the engine runs at 20 Hz, a command applies at a tick boundary); a half tick would need a different simulation. The cost of the shorter turn is twice the messages: 20 turns and 20
  acknowledgements a second per client (about 3 KB/s per client with the WebSocket, TLS and TCP headers), and the server's CPU measured above (12 rooms of 4: 5.7 % of a core with turns of 100 ms, 8.5 % with 50 ms).
* **TCP_NODELAY** is set on every socket (`tcp.cpp`, `ws.cpp`) and `nginx` forwards `/ws` with `proxy_buffering off` (`docker/nginx.conf`): the path adds nothing measurable (1 ms of jitter to beta, turn spacing at the relay sd 1.0 ms).
* The TCP listener's backlog was raised from 8 to 64 (and the WebSocket listener's from 32 to 64) for bursts of connections on a server's ports: done in v0.0.94.
* The browser's frame loop is the largest part of what is left (17 - 21 ms): a page that handles `pointerdown` and sends at once would cut it further (a hidden tab has no frame at all since v0.0.96: the server's messages run it, and nobody sees its readout).

**Tests** (`tests/test_net/test_latency.cpp`, 36 tests; `tests/test_net/test_jitter.cpp`, 26 tests): the pure meters (the window means and medians and their rounding, a ping from Ping / Pong timings with unbelieved answers, the age of a
message taken off a Pong at every frame rate and in a page that was not drawn for 20 s, a reading that goes stale, a slow link with several pings out, a clock that wraps, a command's delay from send to execution, the stamp of a command
sent between frames, identical commands in order, a lost or refused command, the median of five and its dash after ten seconds); a host and a guest over
a simulated link of 40 ms each way (ping 80 ms, delay = 40 + the wait for the seal + 40 + one turn of buffer, exact for all 100 phases of a turn: 130 - 179 ms; the host's own delay 50 - 99 ms; the room's ping), over a link with
jitter; the stall, the rebuilt buffer, the bunch run down, the hitch and the hidden window; and the rule alone: a steady link gives one turn at every frame rate, jitter of 30 / 60 / 120 / 200 ms gives 1 - 2 / 2 - 3 / 3 - 4 / 4 turns,
a stall grows it by one only when one more turn would bridge it, a spike under 5 % of the turns is ignored, a frame over 100 ms is not counted, the shrink is one step per ten seconds and never two together, the speed of the runner
is 4 quarters in the buffer and a quarter more per 50 ms beyond it, 16 at most, and a runner on ten random jittery links learns them in a few seconds and does not stand still for 100 ms after. The review fixes added: the last turn(s) of a stream late by 60 / 120 / 300 ms and 19 shapes of late turns near
the end (J2.9, J2.10), long frames of 150 - 1000 ms that run a backlog down (J2.11), a stall that begins or ends in a long frame (J2.12 - J2.14), `backlog_ms()` in 50 ms turns (J2.15), and a link that freezes once against one that
is jittery (J1.10, J2.16).

## Prediction of one's own orders (`prediction.hpp`, `cue_router.hpp`, `NetGame`, `Application::view_sim`)

**What the player gets.** With the prediction switched on (it is off by default: the switches, below), in a match of the network (a server's room or a LAN game, host or guest) the player's own orders take effect on his own screen at once. Before, an order was only a request: it travelled to the host, waited for the next 50 ms turn to be sealed, travelled back and waited in the jitter buffer, and the picture changed when the tick that runs it had run: the corner's `delay` (83 ms at a round trip of 0, 133 ms at 60 ms, 283 ms at 200 ms) and then the ant's own reaction to the order on top of it. The original has no such wait (every machine simulates its own team). Now the order is also run on a second engine at once, the screen shows that engine, and the ants answer the click as they do in a game of one machine. **Nothing else changes**: the confirmed engine, the host's sealing, the referee of the server, the network protocol (no new number), the rules and every state hash are what they were, and no machine can tell whether another predicts (a match in which one client predicts and another does not stays identical: N3.25).

**The two engines.**

* The **confirmed engine** (`NetGame`'s `sim_`, the application's `sim()`) runs the sealed turns and nothing else. The network, the hashes, the web page's sync hash, the bots, the news, the scorecard and the end of the match read it.
* The **predicted engine** (`net::Prediction`) is **derived state**: a copy of the confirmed engine, plus the turns that are already in hand (the jitter buffer holds turns that the host has sealed and the engine has not run yet: they are KNOWN commands of every player and are run as such, nothing about them is guessed), plus the player's own orders that no turn has carried yet (each put at the display tick), advanced to the **display tick** `D = confirmed tick + lead`. What other players are going to order is never guessed: their ants go on with what the confirmed engine knows. The tests pin the property that makes this safe: the engine that was advanced tick by tick equals the engine that a rebuild would make now (`Prediction::derived_hash`), at every frame, under random jitter, stalls, bursts and orders of every kind from every seat.
* It is **advanced one tick with every tick of the confirmed engine** (the lock-step runner's tick hook), and every live turn that arrives (`LockstepRunner::set_on_turn`) is compared with what the prediction assumed for its tick. Equal (nothing, or the player's own orders where they were put): nothing happens. Different (another player's command that the prediction did not have, an own order that the host sealed at another tick than the one it was put at, a command of a kind that is not predicted): the engine is marked stale and **rebuilt** the next time something asks for it, at most once a frame: a copy of the confirmed engine and a replay up to the display tick. The copy is cheap (1 - 24 us native, up to 170 us in the browser on the shipped maps: `docs/audit/rollback_notes.md` R1) and the ticks of the replay are not (a tick takes 0.01 - 0.06 ms in a match that is not busy and up to half a millisecond in one that is, with spikes of 2 ms for the path searches), so a rebuild costs 0.04 ms in a quiet match and 0.4 - 0.9 ms in a busy one (24 ants walking, several players ordering), and the predicted engine is advanced a tick at a time, not rebuilt every frame.
* **Orders**: `NetGame::submit` sends the order first, then hands it to the prediction, which applies it to the predicted engine in the same call and answers with the engine's own verdict (the ant that answers with its voice, the ants that needed the order, a refusal). Predicted: group moves, group special orders, group attacks and Stop. **Not predicted** (they wait for their turn, as before): Hatch (a new ant needs an id that only the confirmed stream gives), the alliance commands (a dialog with another player), Quit and Drop. An order that no turn ever carries (the host refused it, a host change lost it) is dropped after 100 ticks.

**The lead.** The display has to stand far enough ahead to cover the **lag** of the player's own orders: the ticks from the confirmed tick at which an order is given to the tick that the host seals it into. It is measured on the orders themselves: the lead is the **median lag of the last five orders** (the upper one of two in the middle: a lead that is too low is the worse mistake) that are not older than 400 ticks, so that one order that a stall held up does not move it and a lag that has changed for good does, at once. Until an order has taught it, the estimate from the ping and the jitter buffer stands in. It rises at once, falls by one tick after 40 ticks of being too long (so that the picture does not jump back and forth with the phase of the host's seals) and stays between 1 and 12 ticks. The corner's delay is measured to the moment the applying tick has run, a tick after the lag stops: a round trip of 0 ms has a delay of 83 ms and a lag of 1 tick, 60 ms 133 ms and 2, 200 ms 283 ms and 5.

**No bias.** An order is put at the display tick, so with a lead equal to the lag it lands where the host runs it: nothing is corrected while the lag holds, and the ants answer a click as they do on your own computer (192 ms after the click: the wait for the next tick, 25 ms on average, and the ants' own three-tick start-up, which is the original's). `Prediction::Config::lead_bias_ticks` (0) can lengthen the lead: with 1 the ants react 50 ms sooner than in a local game where the round trip is under about 100 ms, but every order costs a rebuild and about one in six shows a whole-tile hop of an ant that was already walking (a tile step is eight ticks, and a tick earlier or later can be the boundary before or the boundary after). The owner chose the local feel without the hops; `docs/audit/rollback_notes.md` R6 has both measurements.

**What reads which engine.** The match screen reads the predicted engine (`Application::view_sim()`): the frame (the world, the grid, the panel, the minimap, the selection), the HUD's step of every tick, the HUD's input (what a click picks, the group order and its feedback, the rubber band) and the cursor. Everything else reads the confirmed engine: the news (told once, from the confirmed stream), the scorecard and the end of the match, the hashes, the bots, the names. Ant ids are the same in both engines (the predicted engine hatches nothing: a new ant appears when its turn runs, with the id the confirmed engine gives it), so a selection by id means the same ant in both.

**Sounds** (`CueRouter`). Walking has no sound in this game (the walk and idle clips carry none), and the voices of the HUD are instant. The cues that belong to the player's own order, made by an own ant that is doing what it was told (the can't-go cue, the harvest and grab, the two power-up chimes, the fire ant's, the bomber's, the swimmer's bridge, the thief's raid, the bump), and the stop events that cut what that ant started, are played from the predicted engine, in step with the picture; the confirmed engine's copy of the same cue, which comes a few ticks later (the nearest occurrence of the same kind within 12 ticks), is dropped. What a rebuild replays is never played, so a correction cannot make a cue heard twice. Every other cue (combat, hits, deaths, explosions, scores, the alliance cues, the clock's warnings, every cue of another player's ants) is the confirmed engine's, played when it makes them: it is never a guess. A cue that was played for what then did not happen (a phantom) can only be an own ant's own action after a correction; it is counted and costs one short sound.

**When it is off.** Every state in which the confirmed engine does not simply follow the live stream: the pre-start (the "Get ready to play!" dialog of protocol 12: there is no executed turn yet, so no predicted engine), a pause (the runner is held), a catch-up (a server's lag policy), a host change or a rejoin, a desync, a match that is not in its Playing phase, a **hidden web page** (its wake-ups step the match and no picture needs the predicted engine), and a game of one machine (it has no delay). It begins again with the first tick after the cause is gone. **The budget**: a rebuild, or the ticks that a confirmed tick makes the prediction run, that cost more than 12 ms of the thread's CPU time (a sleep, a preempted thread or a stalled process is not its cost; the web build has one thread and counts the time that goes by) are a strike; four strikes within 200 confirmed ticks start a **cool-down**: the prediction is off for 200 confirmed ticks (10 s: the confirmed engine is shown, orders go as they did before, the console says so) and then begins again from the confirmed engine; each further cool-down of the match is twice as long, up to 3200 ticks (160 s). A big community map on a slow device keeps its game and loses the prediction for a while.

**Switches and the readout.** **Off by default** (opt-in, the owner's decision: other players' ants still hop about a dozen pixels when their orders arrive). A match that is not asked for it never makes the second engine (no memory, no ticks, no cue routing: nothing runs); asked for at run time it is made with the next update and begins with the next tick, switched off it is destroyed. `--prediction on` (and `--prediction off`, `--no-prediction`), the settings' key `prediction` (`on` / `off`, absent: off; the command line wins), and `?prediction=on` on the web page's address (which gives the game `--prediction on`; `?prediction=off` says off). While the prediction is on the corner's `delay` shows what the click FEELS (from the frame that took the order to the frame that shows what it did: the median of the last five, a dash until an order has been felt and ten seconds after the last one); with it off, the network's delay as before (the time until the confirmed engine applies the order, which with the prediction on is what the confirmed engine waits, not the picture).

**What it cannot do.**

* An order of another player is not known before its turn arrives. When it does, the ants it concerns are put where the corrected timeline has them: they move by `lead` ticks of their walking at once (4 pixels a tick: 12 pixels on average and a tile at most in the measurement, two ants a time, only the ants that the order concerns; about two of every three foreign orders are seen as such a hop, measured with 24 ants walking, the rest concern ants that were not moving). Their fights, explosions and hits are shown by the predicted engine `lead` ticks ahead of the confirmed one and heard when the confirmed engine makes them: at a round trip of 60 ms (a lead of 2 ticks), 100 ms after their picture.
* Hatching, the alliance commands, Quit and Drop wait for their turn (an egg, a dialog or a leave is not an own ant's reaction to a click).
* **Two small jumps are known and left for the smoothing work** (the review's LOW findings). (1) When the jitter buffer holds more turns than the lead (right after a burst of turns, before the lead has learned the longer lag), an order must be put behind the turns in hand: the picture jumps ahead by the difference at that order and then waits for the confirmed engine to catch up. (2) When the lead changes (it rises at once and falls by one tick after 40 ticks of a lower target), the display moves by one tick of walking at once (a rise runs two ticks in one, a fall stands still for one): about 4 pixels for every ant, once per change.
* A lead that is too low (the first orders of a match before the lag is known on a slow link, a lag that has just grown) is the one visible flaw: the order is put at a tick that the host has already sealed without it, and is chased turn by turn (a rebuild for each) until its own turn comes; the ordered ants hover back and forth for the few ticks that takes. The lead rises at once to what the orders say.
* The second engine costs memory (about 0.12 - 0.37 MiB on the shipped maps: R1's census) and the predicted ticks are CPU work that is done twice (measured: the game's own frame function takes 0.25 - 0.9 ms in the browser with the prediction on or off, in the same run, and a frame of the native application costs the same with and without it; one predicted tick takes 0.01 - 0.06 ms and a rebuild 0.04 - 0.9 ms against the budget of 12 ms).

**Measured** (`docs/audit/rollback_notes.md` R6 has the method, the correction rates and the other numbers). The real application over real TCP sockets with a link of a chosen delay in each direction, in virtual time so that the load of the machine does not move it (60 frames a second, 30 clicks a cell), the time from a right click to the end of the first frame in which the ants stand up (their first visible reaction; the click marker that the HUD draws at the release is in the same frame whatever the network does):

| Round trip | the corner's `delay` without the prediction | the ants stand up, without | the ants stand up, with | the lead |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a game of one machine | - | 192 ms | - | - |
| 0 ms | 83 ms | 242 ms | 192 ms | 1 tick |
| 20 ms | 83 - 133 ms | 243 - 290 ms | 189 ms | 1 tick |
| 60 ms | 134 ms | 294 ms | 192 ms | 2 ticks |
| 100 ms | 183 ms | 342 ms | 192 ms | 3 ticks |
| 200 ms | 283 ms | 444 ms | 192 ms | 5 ticks |

With the prediction the ants answer a click as quickly as in a game on your own computer while the link is steady: 192 ms at every round trip (the frame that takes the click, the wait for the next tick, 25 ms on average, and the ants' own start-up of three ticks, which is the original's). In the browser (the web build in a headless Chrome at 60 frames a second, a native game server behind a relay, two windows, the orders given with the mouse) the corner's `delay` read 84, 149 and 282 ms at round trips of 0, 60 and 200 ms without the prediction and 15, 18 and 16 ms with it (a frame or two, the wait for the next tick; measured when the lead still had a tick of bias, and not repeated: the felt delay does not depend on the lead).

**Tests.** Suite 2.25 / 2.26 `test_engine_copy` (the engine copy, 20 tests and the whole matches of the six shipped maps), suite 2.27 `test_prediction` (a rig that plays the server and an oracle engine: exactness, corrections, the derived-state property under random jitter, the states, the cues, the budget and its cool-downs, the lead), N3.25 - N3.33 in `test_netgame` (real sockets, three machines: one predicts and another does not, a host change, a pause, a desync, the budget and its cool-down, the lead, a default match without any prediction, one made and destroyed on demand), N9.37 - N9.39 in `test_latency` (the felt-delay meter), suite 3.22 `test_prediction_app` (a real application: the frame, the click, the HUD, the switches, the hidden page, the corner, the cue router that idles, the HUD's pointer when the prediction is switched off). Every test of the suites was proven by mutation (`docs/audit/rollback_notes.md`). `tests/scripts/test_web_prediction.sh` (opt-in, like the other browser checks: it needs a running stack and a Chromium-based browser) plays two matches in two windows of a real browser, one with the defaults and one with `?prediction=off`, with the orders given by the mouse, and checks that the prediction is on and predicts, then off and predicts nothing, that the click is felt sooner than the confirmed engine applies it, that the game's frame function stays cheap, and that the state hashes of a window that predicts and one that does not are equal.

## Protocol 6, the WebSocket transport and the pieces of the dedicated server (v0.0.81)

* **Protocol 6** (`protocol.hpp`): a map name may hold any printable ASCII character except `/ \ : * ? " < > |` (the community's names hold spaces, `!`, `~`, `#`, `&`, quotes and even `..`; a name cannot hold a separator and ends in `.lvl`, so none names a path), up to 64 characters (`kMaxMapNameChars`; 348 of 586 community names were refused by the old rule of letters, digits, `_ - .` and 32 characters; the LAN datagram grew to at most 160 bytes); **Hello carries a room code and a token** (`room`: letters, digits, `_`, `-`, up to 32, empty for a LAN or direct host; `token`: up to 64 printable characters without spaces, carried and never interpreted by the game) and a lobby with a room code answers a Hello for another room with `Reject NoSuchRoom`; the slot state `Bot` (a computer player, `docs/BOTS.md`) is allowed in the Room message. Client: `--room CODE`, `--token T`.
* **WebSocket server transport** (`ws.hpp`, native builds; v0.0.81): `WsListener` / `WsConnection` implement `Connection` over RFC 6455 (binary messages, masking checked, fragmentation reassembled up to 64 KB, ping / pong, close handshake, a server ping every 20 s, 1 MB send backlog limit, an 8 KB / 5 s handshake limit, an optional origin allow-list and path), with a socket-free frame parser for fuzzing. It is checked against Node's and Python's WebSocket clients and through an nginx proxy (the header comment has the proxy lines). It is the transport for browsers and Electron: the page connects out to the server, so no NAT traversal is needed. Like the TCP transport, it delivers what a peer sent before it closed the link (the connection ends Closed, `test_ws` W1.16) or, where the kernel keeps it, before it reset it (Failed, W1.26).
* **Control interface primitives** (`ants_ctl`, native builds): a strict JSON library and a minimal HTTP/1.1 server on the loopback address with a bearer secret (constant-time compare, `GET /healthz` without it), with which a lobby backend will create and inspect rooms.
* **Map sweep** (`tools/map_sweep.cpp`, an optional target): plays every map of a folder headless in child processes (crash and hang isolation, three plays per map compared by hash). Over the 586 community maps it found no crash or hang, a determinism defect (a second match on one engine object began with the first match's attack times: `last_attacked_ms_` was not reset by `init`), the undefined float-to-int conversion of one map's flower probabilities (now the original's `__ftol`, 0x1034580: truncate to 64 bits, keep the low 32), and loader / start-marker issues that v0.0.86 fixed: the loader now reads what the original reads (547 of 586 maps load), a start marker outside the grid makes the map unplayable only for rosters that contain that team (`LevelData::validate(roster)`; a room checks it when it is about to start), and the invented 40 x 40 rule is gone.
* **The dedicated server** is described in the next section (v0.0.83).

## The dedicated server (`src/ants_server`, v0.0.83)

Decision (owner, 2026-09-30): **no NAT traversal in the game**. The game has no real lobby of its own; an external lobby launches it, and for internet play a server that everybody can reach hosts the matches. `ants_server` is that server: a headless program, one process, many rooms, no threads (one `update(now)` per pass over the sockets and the rooms, like the rest of the network code).

* **Room** (`room.hpp`): a seatless `HostLobby` (room code, `min_players` = `max_players` = the expected players) that becomes a seatless `HostSession` with its own `SimulationEngine`, the **referee** (`Sequencer::on_referee_hash`: every client's hash of a turn is compared with the referee's own; the room fails and says why when one diverges). It is made from outside (`RoomSpec`: map file, fog, players 2 - 4, code, seed, wait / load / keep times) and starts by itself: when the expected seats are taken the lobby sends Start, every client loads the map and reports, and the match begins when all have. A client that cannot load or that leaves cancels the start (back to Waiting; at most a few tries, with a 2 s back-off so a client that can never load the map does not loop); a room that nobody completes fails after `wait_ms`. Finished and failed rooms are kept for status calls (`keep_ms`) and written to `<code>.json` in `--results-dir`. The result rows are the results screen's (an alliance is one row).
* **The door** (`room_manager.hpp`): every connection (TCP from native clients, WebSocket through a TLS proxy for browsers and Electron) arrives without a room; the first message must be a Hello within 10 s and its room code (protocol 6) names the room. A Hello for a room that does not exist or is over (`NoSuchRoom`: a finished or failed room is not "a match that has started"; a public room that is over is made again by a Hello that carries a create block, "Protocol 15" below), for a match that is running (`MatchRunning`), another protocol version, or anything else is answered with a Reject and the connection is closed after a short linger so the answer arrives. Limits: 256 rooms, 128 connections that have not said Hello.
* **The public status, `GET /busy` (on the WebSocket port)**: `WsServerOptions::status_path` and `status_body` (`ws.hpp`; the server sets them when `--ws-port` is on: `WsListener::set_status`) make the handshake parser answer one plain HTTP request before it looks at the path and the origin of the door: a `GET` of exactly the status path (`/busy`: no query, no trailing slash, case as written) with no `Upgrade` header, exactly one `Host`, and no body or transfer coding gets `200 OK`, `Content-Type: application/json`, `Cache-Control: no-store`, `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `Connection: close` and the text, and the connection closes (`WsHandshakeResult::Status::Answered`; it never becomes a `WsConnection`, so nothing reaches the game). Any other method is 405, a query or another path is no status request (the door's usual 426 / 404), a missing or doubled `Host` or a body is 400, and a text longer than `kWsMaxStatusBytes` (512) is a 500, never a long answer. The text is `{"matches":N,"players":M}` from `RoomManager::busy()`: `matches` counts the rooms whose match is **loading or running with a person in it** (present or catching up; a room that waits for players is no match: a restart would only drop a lobby; a room that a restart brought back also counts for its first 5 minutes, see "Restart records"), `players` the people, **bots not counted**, in rooms that wait or load and the people who are there in a match (a room that is finished, failed or closed holds nobody who plays). No name, no room code, no address and no secret is in it; it costs one pass over the rooms (at most 256) and takes no parameter. `docker/nginx.conf` forwards `https://<site>/busy` to it as an exact location like `/ws`, but GET only (405 otherwise), 404 for a query, no body and no upgrade passed on, one request a second per address with a burst of 5 (503 beyond it) and five-second timeouts. The deploy job of CI polls it before it restarts the site (`docs/WORKFLOW.md`, "Deploy from CI and the staging site"): **a deploy recreates the server's container and ends every match**, and there is no drain mode, so a match that starts in the seconds between the last poll and the restart is still ended (a server that stops taking new rooms before a restart is a later step). Tests: `test_ws` W1.24 (the parser: every method, path, query, header and body case; the longest text; a real socket: the answer arrives whole, the socket closes, the door still opens for a game), `test_server` S3.81 (the counts through a room's whole life: waiting, loading, running, with a bot, closed, finished), `tests/scripts/test_ants_server.sh` (the real server's answers: 0 and 0, one match with two people while a match runs, 0 and 0 again; a POST is 405, a query 426) and `tests/scripts/test_nginx_conf.py` (the proxy block).
* **The site statistics, `GET /stats` and `POST /stats/local` (on the WebSocket port, `site_stats.hpp`)**: the front page shows how many games were played. Two counters, each for the **last 24 hours** (24 buckets of an hour on the server's clock: the hour that runs and the 23 before it) and **in all**, and `since`, the UTC day they count from. **`online`** is a match that ran **at least 600 ticks (30 seconds of play)** and ended, in any room (demo rooms too: every web match is one), counted once when `RoomManager::take_ended` tells its end (`SiteStats::kMinTicks`): a match that the owner closed, a desync or the room's time limit ended counts once it has run that long; a shorter one counts nothing, nor does a room that never began (nobody came, closed while it waited or during the "Get ready" dialog), so a start-and-quit loop cannot pad the number; a match that a stop of the server cuts off is not told as ended and is not counted, and one that the server brings back from a restart record is counted when it ends (its ticks go on from the record's). **`local`** is a single-player game that a browser reports: **the game runs in the tab, so the server cannot see it, and a script can post as well; it is what browsers say, a figure for the page and no proof.** `GET /stats` is answered as `/busy` is (the same door rules; `Cache-Control: no-store`) with `{"now":{"matches":N,"players":M},"online":{"day":D,"total":T},"local":{"day":d,"total":t},"since":"YYYY-MM-DD"}`: `now` is `/busy`'s answer, the rest numbers and one date (it is not the control interface's `GET /stats`, which is behind the secret and never routed). `POST /stats/local` takes **no body and no query** (`WsServerOptions::post_path` and `post_action`: exactly that path, no `Upgrade`, one `Host`, `Content-Length` absent or 0, no `Transfer-Encoding`; another path or method is 405 and `Allow:` says what the path takes, a malformed request 400; the parser stays pure and only says `posted`, the listener runs the action once), counts one game and answers **204**; **at most 120 reports count in any 60 seconds**, the others get the same 204 and are not counted (the cap cannot be seen), and the server keeps nothing of a request but the count (the web server's own access log is what it is for every request). The counters live in **`site-stats.json` of `--results-dir`** (`{"format":1,"since":...,"online":{"total":T,"hours":[[hour number,count],...]},"local":{...}}`, the buckets of the window only), written whole to a temporary file, flushed to the disk (`fsync`, `_commit` on Windows) and then renamed, **at most every 10 s while something changed** and at a clean stop (a write that fails removes the temporary file and leaves the old file whole), read at start; a file that is no usable file (not JSON, a counter that is no number, buckets that hold more than the total, a date that is no day) is put aside as `site-stats.json.broken-<seconds>`, the counters start at 0 and a log line says so; without `--results-dir` they live in memory; a crash loses the last 10 s at most. `docker/nginx.conf` forwards both as exact locations like `/busy`, with **nothing of the visitor's headers** passed on (the server is sent `Host`, `Connection` and, for the POST, `Content-Length: 0`: a cookie never reaches it, however big): `/stats` GET only (405, a query 404), twenty a second per address with a burst of 100, **answered from a cache of 5 seconds** (one entry for everybody, so the game server is asked about once in 5 seconds however many people look; an old answer is served while the new one is fetched and when the server does not answer; the server's `no-store` still reaches the browser); `/stats/local` POST only (405, a query 404), a request that the browser says is cross-site (`Sec-Fetch-Site: cross-site`) is 403 before it counts against the limit (the game page's own report is same-origin), sixty a minute per address with a burst of 20 and **never a body passed on** (the server is given an empty POST; a body over 1 KiB is 413); 503 beyond the limits, five-second timeouts. The limits are sized to hold for the whole site, because behind a reverse proxy that ends TLS every visitor may have the proxy's address (the server's own cap of 120 a minute is the bound that matters). **The web game reports its own games**: `Application::set_on_local_match_started` is told at the first tick of a game on this computer (once for each match, never in a match of the network; only the web build sets it, to a call of the page's `antsReportLocalGame()` in `web/shell.html`: one `fetch('/stats/local', {method: 'POST', keepalive: true})`, no retry, every failure ignored); a native game reports nothing. Tests: `test_ws` W1.25 (the door), `test_server` S3.140 - S3.149 (the counting, the window, the cap, the file, the answer, a real manager and a real listener), `tests/scripts/test_ants_server.sh` (the real program, its rooms part), `tests/scripts/test_nginx_stats.py` (the blocks, the limits and the cache, run in front of a stand-in server, and in front of the real program), `test_app_integration` 7.6h and `test_network_app` N5.90 (the hook), `tests/scripts/web_report_check.js` and `test_web_report.py` (the page's function); the mutants of all of it: `docs/audit/site_stats_notes.md`.
* **The replays of the rooms' matches (`replay_store.hpp`, `control.cpp`; [`REPLAYS.md`](REPLAYS.md) "On the game server", the options and numbers in [`SERVER.md`](SERVER.md#replays))**: the referee's runner calls the room's recorder after every turn it executes (`LockstepRunner::set_on_executed`: observation only, no hash changes). When the match is over, or the room failed or was closed, a match that ran 600 turns (30 seconds) however it ended is encoded (`replay::Recorder::finish`) and handed to the `ReplayStore`, which keeps it as `ants-<MAP>-<YYYYMMDD>-<HHMMSS>Z[-<n>].antsrep` (the UTC time the match ended) in one folder of the results volume. A seat of a person has the name that the room showed everybody (`Room::replay_begin`; `replay_person_name`: printable ASCII, 32 characters, the blanks at both ends cut), except that the words that the game itself uses for a player without a name ("Player", "Player 1" to "Player 4") and an empty name leave the seat without one, and the readers show its colour; a bot's seat has its display name; no address, room code, key or chat is in it, and the names are public wherever the public door is on. The store touches only files of that form, reads the age of a file from its name, finds, reads and deletes a file by a name from its own index (never by a path from outside), and writes it whole or not at all (a temporary name, then a rename, never a replace). A room that was brought back from a restart record does not record. The control interface lists, gives and deletes the files behind the secret; the second door, `--replay-port` (`ctl::HttpServer::listen_public`: no secret, GET only), gives `GET /replays` and `GET /replays/<file>` and nothing else, and `docker/nginx.conf` passes those two addresses to it with limits of its own and a short cache of the list. Tests: `tests/test_server/test_replay_store.cpp` (the store), S3.160 - S3.175 in `tests/test_server/replay_tests.hpp` (whole matches, played again from the file to the same hash, restarts, demo rooms, the routes and the public door, the names that are kept and the ones that are not), CTL2.22 and CTL2.23 (the read-only HTTP door), `tests/scripts/test_nginx_replays.py` (the nginx blocks, read and run).
* **The map store** (`map_store.hpp`): the folder of `.lvl` files from `--maps`; a name is only a name in the folder (the protocol's `valid_map_name`: never a path), a room is refused (404 / 400) when the map is missing or does not load, so a room that exists can always be played. The Docker image carries the six maps of the original game in `/maps`; a folder mounted there replaces them (the lobby keeps the library and syncs it).
* **The control interface** (`control.hpp`, HTTP + JSON, `ants_ctl`): `POST /rooms`, `GET /rooms`, `GET /rooms/<code>`, `DELETE /rooms/<code>`, `GET /stats`, `GET /replays[?limit=N]`, `GET /replays/<file>`, `DELETE /replays/<file>` (the kept replays, below), every call behind the bearer secret (constant-time compare). The secret is never read from the command line (a command line is visible to every user): it is `ANTS_SERVER_SECRET`, or, when that is not set, a random secret (32 bytes of the operating system's generator, 64 hex digits) that the server makes the first time it starts and keeps in a file (`--secret-file`, default `control-secret` in `--results-dir`, an explicit `--secret-file` wins) (`src/ants_server/secret.cpp`). It is shown in the log once, the moment it is made (before the ports are opened), and read from the file at every later start. The file is made all at once: the complete secret goes to a temporary file next to it, which is linked to the final name (POSIX `link()`, Windows `MoveFileEx` without replacing), so servers that start at the same moment share one secret and a crash leaves no half-written file. On POSIX only its owner can read it (mode 600); **on Windows the server sets no permissions and the file takes those of its folder**. A file that already exists is used as it is (its owner and mode are the operator's responsibility, a symbolic link is followed); one that is no usable secret (not a regular file, empty, outside 32 - 256 characters, a space or control character, more than one line, bigger than 1,024 bytes) stops the server and is never overwritten. It listens on the loopback address; a deployment that needs more says so explicitly (`--ctl-any-interface`, for a container whose host maps the port to its own loopback address). The lobby's backend calls it; room codes first, per-player tokens (the Hello carries one already, the game never interprets it) when the lobby wants to check who joins.
* **Clients**: the same game, `--join HOST:PORT --room CODE` (a Hello with the room code; a lobby launches it with the code). `NetGame` recognises a server's room by its **missing Host slot**: no host migration (a client whose server link dies loses the match, it does not elect itself the host of a one-player game; the server drops the seat at the same tick for everybody else), and the match starts when the room says so: when every seat is taken, or, since protocol 7, when the room's **leader** (the first player who joined) presses START (below). Everybody else's screen waits.
* **A room never waits for a seat that lags (protocol 8)**: the room's host has no seat, so it keeps sealing a turn every 50 ms whatever lag a seat has (a room that HOLDS seats, protocol 10, pauses for a seat whose connection is LOST, and for nothing else: "Reconnect", below); a seat that falls behind is announced to the others (`Lag`, "Bob is lagging (12 s behind)") from 3 s, catches up alone at up to 4x ("Catching up..."), and is dropped at 60 s behind or after 30 s without an acknowledgement (the old rule held the room up for 3 s and then for 20 s). `RoomStatus::turns` is one per tick now (turns of 50 ms). See "What the player feels: ... Who waits for whom".
* **Deployment**: `Dockerfile.server` (debian:12-slim, builds the program alone with `-DANTS_BUILD_APP=OFF`, runs unprivileged, healthcheck = a connection to the game port), `docker-compose.server.yml` (example). Only the TCP port is public; the WebSocket port goes behind the nginx that ends TLS (the proxy lines are in the header of `ws.hpp`), the control port is for the lobby's backend only. Capacity: a match costs about 83 microseconds of one core per tick on average (map sweep, 586 maps), so a core holds hundreds of games; the memory of a room is the engine (a few MB).
* **Tests**: suite 2.19 (`tests/test_server`, tests over real sockets and real files: the store, the rooms' whole life, the door, the control calls, public rooms (S3.10, S3.23 - S3.25: off unless asked for; a Hello's create block chooses the map and the seats; the ten-minute wait; a code whose public room is over makes a new room when the Hello brings a block; a room of the control interface that is over (closed by its owner) answers `NoSuchRoom`), the server's clock across the 32-bit wrap, a seat that stops executing turns (S3.12) and a leader whose window stops after the early START (S3.33: the room does not wait, the other player is told, its late clicks on START are free, it catches up), the wall-clock limit, and the control secret (S3.15 - S3.22: made once and for its owner only, every kind of bad file, the generator's strength, a file too big to read, symbolic links, eight starts at the same moment, a start that dies), 3.9 (`tests/scripts/test_ants_server.sh`: the real server and two headless game clients, the startup refusals (`--demo-maps` too), public rooms made on the map and for the number of seats that their Hellos' create blocks choose, and the control secret that the server makes), and S2.x in suite 2.11 (the referee).
* **The browser as a client (v0.0.84)**: `WasmWsConnection` (`wasm_ws.hpp`, Emscripten only, `-lwebsocket.js`) is a `Connection` over the page's own WebSocket; `NetGame::join_url` / `--join-url ws[s]://...` joins through it, and the guest branch of `begin_match` is compiled in the web build (no peer listener, no links between guests: a server's room has no Host slot, so migration is off). The page reads `?join=/ws&room=&seat=&name=[&embed=1][&aspect=16:9|4:3]` (`web/shell.html`; `join` must be a path on the same site, everything is validated before it becomes an argument). **`name` is the name that was chosen on the page that made the address** (web/lobby.html always puts the parameter in for its own frames, windows and Join button, empty when none was chosen: the game says Player); **an address of a join that has no `name` parameter at all is a link that somebody shared, and the game page asks for the name before the game starts (so before it connects)**: `ANTS_PAGE.asksForName`, never for `embed=1`, a card over the page with the name that the browser remembered (`ants.name`), a Join button and Enter; the rules of a name are the desktop start menu's (printable ASCII, at most 32 characters, nothing that starts with "Bot ("), and a game that was given a name loses the parameter from the address bar, so that a copy of its address asks too (`web/lobby.html` shows a name only as text and puts it into a link with `encodeURIComponent`; `tests/scripts/web_name_check.js`); nginx passes `/ws` to the server's WebSocket port (`docker/nginx.conf`: the backend name is resolved per request, so the site starts without the server).
* **The front page: the lobby at `/` (`web/lobby.html`, `docker/nginx.conf`, `web/shell.html`)**: the owner: "when you click the play online button it opens in a new tab can we just make that the default. you can play single on the play online tab by setting to 1 player consolidate them". **Routes** (`docker/nginx.conf`; `tests/scripts/test_nginx_routes.py` asks the real nginx, the CI asks the built image with `tests/scripts/web_routes_check.py`): `/` is `lobby.html` unless the query has `join=<something>` (`$arg_join`: a match on the game server, every link that the page or anybody shared): those keep opening the game page `index.html`, as `/` always did (an internal `rewrite`: the answer has the html block's `Cache-Control: no-cache, must-revalidate`, the two cross-origin headers and the ETag, so a conditional request is answered 304); `/four.html` with any query is a permanent redirect to `/` with the same query (`?room=` and `?aspect=` work at `/`; the parameters of the earlier pages, `?map=`, `?players=`, `?fill=` and `?play=here`, are ignored there); the game page also answers at a path of its own, `/play.html` (a copy of `index.html` in the image), for the games on this computer. **The lobby** (the owner's pictures, cards 1 to 18; `web/front/lobby_net.js` is the page's client of "Protocol 16" below, `web/front/lobby_rules.js` the pure rules and every word that the page shows, `web/lobby.html` the screen): there is no separate game against the computer, no separate host and join, and no card to fill in first: the page **is a room**. A plain visit makes a room (a new code of six random letters and numbers, "Protocol 15" below, and a Hello whose create block names the map) and seats the visitor in the first colour as the host, under `ants.name` or a name that the page picked; the room's code and the seat's key are kept by the tab (`sessionStorage`, `ants.lobby`), so a reload takes the seat back, and a tab that was duplicated holds the same seat: when the copy takes it, the first says "This room was opened in another window, so it is closed here." with a button, **Use this window**, that takes the room back (a new tab makes a room of its own). `/?room=<code>` (no create block) is somebody's room: a name card first ("Join the room k7m 2xq", the remembered name, **Join**, a way out, **Start a room of my own instead**), then a Hello that only joins (`joinFirst: true`, no block: a link never makes a room, so a code that no room has, a typo or a link that was cut off, is answered `NoSuchRoom` and the page makes a room of its own with a new code under the strip "There is no room with that code, so this one is yours now. Send the link on if you like."; once the room has taken the page, a room that is lost later, a restart, is made again with the block, as for any page, and the strip says so); **Have a code?** asks the same card for a typed code (blanks and capitals ignored, 1 to 32 of `[a-z0-9_-]`) and sends a Hello that only joins (`join: true`, no block), so a code that no room has gives the notice "There is no room with that code. Check it, or ask the host to send the link again." (the page never makes a room for a code it was given). **The colours** lie Black (top left), Green (top right), Red (bottom left), Blue (bottom right), `GRID = [3, 0, 1, 2]`; a colour's number is still its seat (Green 0, Red 1, Blue 2, Black 3), so a new room fills Green, Red, Blue, Black; the cards are built in reading order, so Tab and a screen reader follow the picture. Each shows a person (**Host**, **You**), **Open**, a computer player (**Easy**, **Medium**, **Hard**) or **Nobody**; the host sets an empty colour with its drop-down (`PLAN` in `lobby_net.js`: Open, Easy, Medium, Hard, Nobody), **drags** a player onto another colour (a free colour is taken, a taken colour swaps; tap the dots and then the colour on a phone; `SeatMove` with the seating hash of what the host saw, so the room ignores a move that is stale), **removes** a player (`Remove`: a question first, then the colour opens and the player gets a room of its own with a green note), and sets **Team 1** and **Team 2** under the colours that play when three or four do (the rules of the older card: two on the same button are a team, with four the other two are the other team and show their button lit, with three the third plays alone; a full button refuses with a line; `sidesFix`, `sideOpen`, `teamsText` in `lobby_rules.js`). A player's screen is read-only (the teams are tabs) but for the **pencil** beside its own name (`Name`, protocol 16: a name that `nameCheck` refuses is explained and not sent). **The map** (the six with a preview and Map Info, **Treasure** until the host chooses) and **START!** are beside the colours; the line under START! says who plays and what an empty colour does. **The link** is `/?room=<code>` (**Copy link**, and **Share** where the browser has `navigator.share`); it carries no name, no key and no create block. **START!** (`StartRequest`): the room is frozen ("Getting ready…", each colour "Opening the game…" and then "In the game"), every page writes the key of its seat (`ants.rejoin.<room>.<seat>`, the game's own rejoin entry, `client.rejoinEntry()`) and goes to `/?join=/ws&room=<code>&seat=<n>&name=<name>&aspect=<shape>`, with no key in the address, and **the server starts the match when every person is in a game** (`kRoomStarting`, the `in_game` mask) and seats the plan's bots; nobody presses START in the games, so the page gives no `--start-when`. A game that is not in within 90 seconds ends the wait and everybody is back in the room, as they left it, with the notices of "Protocol 16". **A game for one** (nobody else in the room and no computer player: every other colour Open or Nobody): START! leaves the room and takes this tab to `/play.html?map=<key>&name=<name>&aspect=<shape>` (no room, no server, no bots, no teams; the game's `--map PATH --play --alone`: only your colony is on the map, You at seat 0, Green, and the line under START! says so), so the match begins after the loading screen and the quick help and plays as the original's single player does. **The leader's game starts the match** only in a game page that is opened with `&start=<people>` (nothing on the front page writes it any more; the test room that did is gone): there the first game to connect leads the room, and its `--start-when N` presses START by itself once N people are in. **Remembered**: the name under `ants.name`, the picture's shape under `ants.aspect.v2`, the room under `ants.lobby` (this tab only); the keys of the earlier cards (`ants-match`, `ants-match-room`, `ants-solo-*`, `ants-four-*`) are not read or written any more. **The strip** at the top of the page shows the notices (green: the page changed something for you; gold: something did not work); the page's sentences are `TEXT` in `lobby_rules.js`, the ones that start from the server's own words repeat what it said. **Old addresses**: `/play.html?map=<key>&bots=...` (a game on this computer) still works, and START! uses it for a game for one; `/?map=<key>&players=...`, `/?room=<code>&roommap=...` and `&play=here` were the test room that the **More ways to play** button opened, which is gone: they are the lobby like any other address (`/?room=<code>` is a link to a lobby room, whatever else follows it). **The line of numbers** in the footer (the block `STATS` of the page): the page asks its own site for `/stats` (`{"now":{"matches","players"},"online":{"day","total"},"local":{"day","total"},"since"}`: games played are online plus local, today is the two days) and, when that does not answer 200 with that shape, for `/busy` (the live part alone); with neither there is no line and no error text; it looks at once and every 30 s while the page is visible (not while it is hidden), with no cache and no cookies, and shows the numbers as text only. **Rejoin**: while this browser holds a fresh key of a seat on this site's game server, a strip at the top of the page (above its header) says "Your match in room CODE is still running: go back to your seat." and has one button, "Rejoin it" (the blocks `REJOINKEY` and `REJOIN`; "The front page's Rejoin button", below). **The look**: the pictures are in `web/front/` (made from the game's own assets by `tools/front_page_art/`), the font is Libre Franklin (SIL OFL, a copy of the game's own in the same folder). **The game page's local parameters** (`ANTS_PAGE.localArguments`, a whitelist like `joinArguments`, read only by a page that joins no server): `map` is one of the six shipped maps by its key (the file name comes from a table), `bots` one level word (all three seats) or three words for seats 1, 2, 3 (`none|easy|medium|hard`, all or nothing; the game's `--bot S:<level>` for each seat that has a bot: the player is seat 0), `teams` `ffa` or `0+N` for a seat N that has a bot (the game's `--teams 0+N`), `name` printable ASCII (when the address names a map or bots and no name, the name that the browser remembered under `ants.name`: a reload keeps it, the page takes the name out of its address bar); no text of the address reaches the game's arguments (`tests/scripts/web_lobby_check.js` runs 20000 random addresses against that), and an address with none of them is today's front page for the game: no argument. **`--play`** (`Application`, `ApplicationConfig::play_at_once`): with `--map PATH` the page gives the game `--map PATH --play` (and `--alone` when no seat has a bot): the game goes through its loading screen and the quick help as ever and starts the map at the setup screen's first visit through its START path (`start_game`: the "Get ready" dialog, the start sound, the music, `--bot`'s seats); `--map` alone is the tests' and the screenshots' direct start, which has none of that. Only the first visit is skipped, and only for a game of this machine without the start menu (a room and the menu are untouched). **Menu**: the game page's header button and footer link go back to `/` in the same tab, with a confirm while a match runs or a room is joined. Nothing opens a new tab by default (the links to the sprites, the changelog, GitHub and the feedback keep theirs). **Checks**: `tests/scripts/test_web_lobby.py` with `web_lobby_check.js` (the code the page keeps for the games on this computer, the game page's whitelists, and that every member of the two front scripts that the page uses is there), `test_web_lobby_rules.py` with `web_lobby_rules_check.js` (the rules and every word of the pictures, on tables and a seeded random run, with mutation tests that show the check can fail), `web_lobby_net_check.js` and `web_lobby_client_check.js` (the codec and the client of protocol 16), `web_name_check.js` (the name rules and, with a fake DOM, the real lobby as a whole: a plain visit, the pencil, a link and a typed code asking for a name first, START's hand-off to the game page), `web_rejoin_block_check.js` (the strip), `web_fill_check.js` (the whitelists of `&fill=`, `&teams=` and `&start=`), `test_nginx_routes.py` (needs docker), `tests/scripts/test_web_front.py` (the pictures and the font, the folder's size, the tool, the layout's breakpoints, the colours' contrast), `test_web_stats.py` with `web_stats_check.js` (the line of numbers), the application cases N5.83 - N5.85 (a leader at any colour with `--start-when`, a bot at Green, a friend who leads) and `tests/scripts/test_web_home.sh` (opt-in, real headless Chrome and a game server: see its header).
* **`web/lobby.html`** ("Play online", v0.0.90; the page as it was until v0.12.0: its frames, its windows and their sync reports went with the test room, the server's demo rooms below did not): host a room (a map of the six, Treasure preselected until a choice is remembered, and 2 - 4 players, which the code carried until protocol 15 (`demo-<map>-<n>p-<random>`) and the create block of the Hello carries now) or join one by its code; each seat on the page (a frame of the game page, `embed=1`), in a window of its own, or by a link for somebody else. Before v0.0.90: four frames, seats 0 - 3, one room (`demo-<random>` unless `?room=` names a room made some other way), a 2 x 2 grid. Each game posts `{ants:'sync', seat, tick, hash}` to its parent every 100 ticks (`Application::post_tick`, web build only), or, in a window of its own, on the `BroadcastChannel` "ants-sync" of the site with its room code, and the page compares the hashes of the same tick. **Demo rooms** (`--demo-rooms N --demo-map NAME [--demo-maps LIST]`, `RoomManager::make_public_room`): a Hello with a create block for a room that does not exist makes it (ten minutes to fill (`ServerLimits::demo_wait_ms`; one minute before v0.0.90, which is too short for friends on other computers), 30 s of keep time, at most 30 minutes of play), at most N at a time; off by default. Since protocol 15 the create block chooses the map (one of `--demo-maps`, else `--demo-map`) and the 2 - 4 players; before it the code did (`demo-[<map>-][<n>p-]<anything>`, 4 players on `--demo-map` for a code with neither) (the server has no default of its own: `--demo-map` is required with `--demo-rooms`; `docker-compose.stack.yml` passes `${ANTS_DEMO_MAP:-TREASURE.LVL}`, the map that is played most, and the commented example of `docker-compose.server.yml` the same). A running room refuses every Hello (`MatchRunning`): nobody is removed, nobody new comes in. A Hello that carries a create block for the code of a demo room that is over (finished or failed) forgets that room (its end is reported as ever) and makes a new one at once: a friend who comes late, a reload, a rematch with the same link; the same Hello for a room of the control interface that is over answers `NoSuchRoom` (before v0.0.90: `MatchRunning`). The page keeps `?room=<code>` in the address bar (`history.replaceState`), so a reload comes back to the room's panel. Verified in the built-in browser against the real images (web, server, nginx proxy): four wasm games and a native referee agree on every hash, orders travel through the server.
* **The page's picture (widescreen milestone M5, `web/shell.html`, `web/lobby.html`)**: the game page shows a 16:9 picture by default and offers the original's 4:3, 16:10 and 21:9; the page passes the choice to the game as `--aspect 16:9|4:3|16:10|21:9` (the desktop's own option: the game's canvas is 960 x 540, 640 x 480, 960 x 600 or 1260 x 540, `Application::choose_aspect`, `kWebDefaultAspect` is 16:9). **Which shape**: the address `?aspect=16:9`, `?aspect=4:3`, `?aspect=16:10` or `?aspect=21:9` (the first one if repeated; anything else is ignored), else the choice the page's selector ("Classic 4:3 / 16:10 / 16:9 / 21:9" under the game) remembered in `localStorage` ("ants.aspect.v2", written by the two selectors only, the game page's and web/lobby.html's, read in a try / catch: a private window remembers nothing; the first versions of the pages used "ants.aspect", which is no longer read: a browser that had remembered a Classic 4:3 under it starts in 16:9 once, and its player picks the classic picture again if wanted), else 16:9 on every device, a phone held upright included (there is no exception for portrait phones since v0.1.0; the page only shows them a hint to turn the phone sideways for a bigger picture, and a remembered 4:3 still wins there). The selector remembers the choice and reloads the page with `?aspect=` (the other parameters of the address, the room and the seat, stay; a page that is in a room that is still waiting, or in a match that is being played here (`ants_match_running()`, exported by the game: the match screen is up and its results are not), asks first: "Leave the match to change the picture?"; a page of a joined match that has begun, whose browser holds the key of its seat, asks nothing and reloads like any reload, so the player comes back to the same match). The lobby's selector has the same four shapes (Classic 4:3, 16:10, 16:9 the default, 21:9; a "fills your screen" tag stands under the one nearest to a computer's screen, with a mouse only): it reads and writes the same key, and its START hands every game `&aspect=` of its choice. **The boxes**: `#game-stage` is the slot (CSS: width `min(100%, 1280 px, (100dvh - --chrome) x 16 / 9)`, `--chrome` = the height of the header, the bar and the paddings, measured by the page; 960 px and 4:3 for the classic picture; the other shapes have rules of their own for 16:10 and 21:9) and the element that goes fullscreen; `#game-container` is the picture's box inside it, sized by the page (`ANTS_PAGE.fitBox`) to a whole number of canvas steps (16 x 9 device pixels, 4 x 3 for the classic picture, 8 x 5 for 16:10, 7 x 3 for 21:9) with the largest that fits, centred. **Why exact pixels**: the game's window system (SDL's Emscripten driver) sets the canvas to `floor(CSS size x devicePixelRatio)` of the canvas's box on every `resize` of the window (measured: a box of 1000 x 562.5 CSS pixels gave a canvas of 1000 x 562, 853.33 x 480 gave 853 x 480, at 1.5x 853.33 x 480 gave 1279 x 720) and takes its first size from the box too; the game then draws its logical canvas into the real one with the largest scale that fits, centred, with black bars where the shapes differ, so a canvas of the wrong shape by a pixel has a black line of its own. With a box of exactly 16 x 9 steps the canvas has the picture's shape exactly and no bar of the game's shows. A CSS size that is exact stays exact (a device ratio with at most six binary places, 1, 1.25, 1.5, 2, 2.625, 3 ..., makes exact products with a size that is a multiple of 1/64, which is a whole or a half pixel, so that the floor cannot lose a pixel: 1600 / 1.25 = 1280), any other size gets a quarter of a device pixel more (`ANTS_PAGE.cssFor`) which the floor drops and the layout's rounding (1/64 px in Chrome and Safari, 1/60 in Firefox) cannot take away. **The bridge**: SDL listens to the window's `resize` event only (and reads the device ratio at that time: measured, a change of the ratio changes nothing until the next `resize`), and a fullscreen toggle, a header that wraps, or a move to a screen of another density does not always send one (reproduced in the research for the page). The page observes the slot, the header and the bar (`ResizeObserver`), the fullscreen change (`fullscreenchange`, `webkitfullscreenchange`: again after 150 and 600 ms, as browsers settle at different times), the window's `resize` and the device ratio (`matchMedia("(resolution: Xdppx)")`), sizes the box, and sends a `resize` of its own whenever the box's device pixels change. **The pointer**: the browser's mouse position reaches the game through Emscripten's SDL as `trunc( trunc(clientX - trunc(box.left)) x floor(box.width) / box.width )` (library_html5.js stores `clientX - (rect.left | 0)` as an integer; SDL 2.28's `Emscripten_HandleMouseMove` scales by `window->w / client_w`, and `window->w` is the CSS width cut to a whole number), and the same for y. Two terms of it move the reading by up to a pixel: the cut of the box's position (a box at 68.98 read one pixel too high) and, for a box whose CSS size is not a whole number, the scale `floor(width) / width`, a hair under 1, which turns every whole offset t into t - 1 (a box of 720.19 read one pixel low across the whole box). `ANTS_PAGE.snapOffset` says where the box must stand: a box whose size is whole (scale exactly 1) on a whole pixel, any other 1/64 px (the layout's step) short of one, which puts back the pixel that the scale takes away; `snapBox()` shifts the box by that (at most half a pixel, with `left` / `top`) after every layout and on every scroll. Measured with the real mouse (the hover edges of the setup screen's START button, `--pointer`): before, up to 0.94 CSS px off (1280 x 720 at 1x: top and bottom -0.94; 1536 x 864 at 1.25x: +0.94; 1000 x 640 at 1x: left and right -0.94); after, 0.06 (the bisection's own step) at every layout (1280 x 720 at 1x, 1000 x 640 at 1x, 1536 x 864 at 1.25x, 1500 x 844 at 1.5x, 1280 x 720 at 2.625x, 844 x 390 at 3x). The box keeps its size, so the picture is as large as before. **Fullscreen**: the stage goes fullscreen, black, and the box is the largest picture of the chosen shape that fits the screen, centred (bars are the stage's black, not the game's); a browser that has no `requestFullscreen` for an element (Safari on an iPhone) or refuses it gets the page's own fullscreen (`#game-stage.pseudo-fullscreen`: fixed over the window, the safe areas respected, a button to leave it; the page behind does not scroll). **The frame cap**: the game's loop is `requestAnimationFrame`; the page wraps it with `ANTS_PAGE.makeFrameLimiter`, which keeps measuring the display (the MEDIAN of the last 24 gaps between display frames: below about 100 Hz nothing is touched, above it the game draws 60 frames a second by a schedule that keeps its phase), so a 144 Hz display does not draw the game 144 times a second at its full resolution. A median, because the smallest gap would cap a jittery 60 Hz display by mistake (a short gap now and then), the first frames of the game (loading) would decide for ever if only the first ones counted, and a long frame of a hidden page is an outlier; a window that moves to a display of another rate is measured again within a dozen frames. **The data download**: the file packager of `index.js` downloads `index.data` (14.7 MB) with an `XMLHttpRequest` whose `onerror` throws, which nothing catches: when several frames of `web/lobby.html` asked for the file at the same moment the browser's cache refused one download (Chromium: `net::ERR_CACHE_WRITE_FAILURE`, reproduced on beta in v0.0.96 and in the built-in browser) and that frame stayed at "Downloading data (0%)..." for ever. `web/shell.html` therefore downloads the file with `fetch` before it loads `index.js` (`Module.getPreloadedPackage(name, size)` is called synchronously by the packager at that moment, with the bytes; if the page has none it falls back to the packager's own download, as a browser without `fetch` does): the first try with the browser's cache (a repeat visit is a 304), up to 3 retries (`ANTS_PAGE.downloadWithRetries`, after 0.4, 1.2 and 3 s) with `cache: 'no-store'` so that a cache entry that cannot be written cannot fail them again, progress as before (the size comes from `Content-Length` when the server sends one, else from the size that the last download left in `localStorage` "ants.datasize"), `index.js` and `index.wasm` asked for at the same time (the cache has them when the game asks; measured, the time to the running game does not change); after the last failure the card says so and has a Reload button (`showDownloadError`, also used for a `NetworkError` that the packager throws itself). **What comes back must be the data** (`ANTS_PAGE.badDownload`): a body whose `Content-Type` is `text/html` (a captive portal answers every address with "200 OK") is refused before it is read, and a body that is not the package's size is refused after it (the exact size, which the Dockerfile writes into the page after the build from `stat` of `index.data`; a build outside the Dockerfile has none and refuses anything under a megabyte); a refusal is a failed try like any other, so the retries and the card take care of it, and the file packager is only handed bytes of exactly the size that it names for exactly `index.data` (`Module.getPreloadedPackage(name, size)`), once. `window.antsDownloadLog` lists the tries. **The program's own files** can fail too, and each puts the card up instead of leaving "Downloading data" on the screen for ever: `index.js` that cannot be loaded (the script's `error`), `index.wasm` that cannot be fetched or compiled (Emscripten leaves that to a promise, `Module.ready`, that nobody listens to: the page's `unhandledrejection` handler, only while the game is not ready and no card is up), and a game that does not start `ANTS_START_TIMEOUT_MS` (60 s) after the data arrived (the watchdog); a game that starts after the card was up takes it away. `index.js` is asked for with a `<link rel="preload">` that the script added after the download takes over, so it is one request with one `?v=` (the Dockerfile's `sed` that adds the build's `?v=` now touches `<script src=index.js>` tags only: it used to add a second `?v=` to the page's own address of the script, and the warm-up request was wasted). **`web/lobby.html`**: the games' frames are 16:9 (4:3 with `?aspect=4:3`, or the choice remembered by the game page's selector, or its own selector; this page's selector has the same four shapes, 16:9 the default, and shows a remembered 16:10 or 21:9 as chosen), each game is told the shape (`&aspect=` on the frame's address and on the "Open a window" links; links for other people carry it only when this page's address names it); the seats keep their places by colour; a window that has room for two frames side by side at the picture's own size (2030 px for 960 x 540, 1390 px for the classic 640 x 480) shows each frame at exactly that size, one canvas pixel for each pixel of the screen, so that the quick help and every text are sharp (a size that is not a whole multiple of the picture's cannot be: in a narrower window the frames fill their column as before, about 651 x 366 CSS px, and the picture is scaled by about 2/3; half the picture's size, 480 x 270, would be too small to read). **Checks**: `tests/scripts/test_web_aspect.sh` (opt-in, not part of `./run_tests.sh`; needs a running web page and a Chromium-based browser) runs the page's logic (`ANTS_PAGE`: the shape of the address, the box's rules over 64,000 boxes (2,016 areas, 16 device ratios, both shapes, including the layout's rounding), the frame cap at 30 - 360 Hz and over a hidden gap), the page at desktop and phone sizes at device ratios 1 to 3 (the canvas has exactly the picture's shape and fills the box, the box fits the window, no sideways scroll), the address and the selector, resizing, fullscreen (the browser's and the page's own) and the pointer (the game draws its own cursor at the position it reads: two screenshots with the pointer at two places show it there; the box stands where the pointer is exact, and the edges of START found with the real mouse are where the game's arithmetic puts them at six layouts), the selector in a running match (the picture changes at once, nothing is asked, the match goes on), the mouse-wheel zoom (`--wheel`: the wheel, a ctrl + wheel and Safari's pinch are cancelled over the canvas only, the title, the selector and the guide scroll the page as usual, and in a running match the wheel zooms the game to 0.5, 1 and 2, the middle button goes back to 1), the loading faults (`index.js` or `index.wasm` failing, a game that never starts, a web page, a small body, an error status, a cut or a one-byte-short body for the data, the package handed over once, a late promise failure, `index.js` asked for once); the script's own exit statuses are checked first (`--exit-codes`: a page that is not there is a skip, a page that hangs is a failure: a hang or a crash is never a skip); the web build's 16:9 default is pinned by a unit test (`kWebDefaultAspect`, suite 3.12).
* **The pointer in fullscreen (edge-pan, `web/shell.html`, "The pointer in fullscreen: the bars, and the pointer lock")**: the owner's report: on a 16:10 screen the 16:9 picture has bars above and below in fullscreen and "if I go off the edge of the game, it no longer pans" (left and right were fine: the picture fills the width). **What was wrong**: the stage, not the canvas, is the fullscreen element, so a pointer over a bar is over the stage: the browser sends the game's canvas nothing (SDL's Emscripten driver listens on the canvas), and the canvas's `mouseleave` becomes SDL_WINDOWEVENT_LEAVE, which sets the game's `pointer_outside_` (no more scrolling: the last position must not keep the map scrolling); measured with the real game in headless Chrome (`ants_probe`): the pointer in the top or bottom bar is gone for the game and the view stays. (SDL 2.28.4 also drops the FIRST leave of a page's life: its window starts with the mouse focus but with `SDL_WINDOW_MOUSE_FOCUS` clear, and `SDL_SendWindowEvent` drops a LEAVE that finds the flag clear; measured as flag 0 and focus 1 right after the game is ready. So the baseline sometimes kept scrolling on the first trip into a bar, and every trip after it stopped.) **The bars**: in fullscreen a pointer over a bar is at the nearest edge pixel of the picture, as in the desktop game, where the window maps the bars to positions outside the picture that `clamp_pointer_event` holds to the edge pixel (the native clamp needed no change; `tests/test_app/test_canvas_layout.cpp` pointer group and `test_app_integration.cpp` 7.8f pin the owner's 16:10 screens 1440 x 900 and 1512 x 982, a real fullscreen window of the dummy driver and the 4:3 picture's pillarbox). The page (1) hands the game a `mousemove` at the clamped pixel for every pointer event over the stage outside the box (`ANTS_PAGE.edgePixel` is the pixel that Emscripten reads, `trunc(clientX - trunc(left))` held to `0 .. floor(size) - 1`: the position `size` would be a pointer that left SDL's window; `ANTS_PAGE.clientFor` is the client position that is read as exactly that pixel; mouse and pen only: a tap's emulated mouse events are not a pointer over a bar), (2) does not pass on the canvas's `mouseleave` towards the stage (a capture listener on the window, so it comes before SDL's whenever that was added) and reports a leave of the canvas when the pointer really goes (the stage's `mouseleave`: another monitor; the page's fullscreen left), and (3) leaves a click, a wheel and a context menu over a bar to the stage (the wheel and the menu are cancelled there). The first pointer that the page hands over is preceded by a leave of the same pixel, which takes SDL's focus away and gives it back (an ENTER sets the flag), so that SDL's flag and focus agree from then on and a leave that the page hands over later is delivered. The browser's arrow is hidden over the fullscreen stage (`cursor: none`: the game draws its own cursor at the edge). The windowed page has its own margin (next entry). **The pointer lock**: on a Mac the Dock and the menu bar come up whenever the pointer touches the screen's edge, even in browser fullscreen, and a page can only prevent that by locking the pointer. The click on the Fullscreen button asks for the lock (`canvas.requestPointerLock()` in the same gesture; once more at `fullscreenchange`; a click on the game asks again where the browser let it go, and where the page has held the lock before that click is only that, the game does not get it) when the browser's own fullscreen is made (not the page's own: an iPhone has neither) and the setting is on. While the pointer is locked the browser sends the canvas relative motion (`movementX / movementY`, the position frozen): the page swallows that (a capture listener on the window stops it before SDL's), keeps the game's own cursor (`ANTS_PAGE.lockStart`: the middle of the picture, or the click's position when a click took the lock; `ANTS_PAGE.moveLocked`: the same CSS pixels as the mouse moved, fractions kept, held to the picture) and hands the game that cursor as ordinary absolute motion, so every edge and corner scrolls from the clamped cursor, the way back from an edge is at once, and a click goes where the cursor is. **Why not SDL's own relative mode** (the page requests the lock itself; SDL's `pointerlockchange` listener sets `has_pointer_lock` for ANY lock, and SDL would then treat all motion as relative): Emscripten's `fillMouseEventData` stores `movementX` as an int32 (a browser that reports fractions would lose them on every event), SDL's relative motion needs the mouse focus and a start position that the page has to supply anyway, and a model in the page is a pure function that `tests/scripts/web_edge_check.js` runs (1000 steps of 0.1 px; the edges, corners and the way back; random motion in six sizes) while SDL's is not ours to test; so the page swallows the browser's `pointerlockchange` (window capture, before SDL's listener on the document) and SDL never hears of the lock. When the lock ends (`exitPointerLock`, Esc in a browser without the Keyboard Lock API, another window with the focus, fullscreen left) the pointer is the normal one at once: nothing is handed to the game (its cursor stays where it was: no jump, no click) and the first report of the free pointer over a bar, where it was when the lock began, is not acted on until it has moved by a pixel. **Esc**: in fullscreen with the setting on the page calls `navigator.keyboard.lock(['Escape'])` (Chromium; it needs a secure context and fullscreen) and `unlock()` when fullscreen ends: Esc then reaches the game and HOLDING it leaves fullscreen (and the lock), as the browser says; elsewhere Esc releases the lock first (Safari leaves fullscreen with it). **The setting**: "Fullscreen mouse: Locked / Free" next to the picture's selector (`.seg button[data-lock]`), remembered in `localStorage` under `ants.pointerlock` (`on` / `off`; only `off` turns the lock off, `ANTS_PAGE.lockSetting`; the control is not shown where there is no `requestPointerLock`); Free also leaves Esc to the browser; the bars' rules are the same in both. **For the tests** the game exports `ants_probe(what)` (read-only: the pointer's x, y, whether it is gone, the view's origin, whether a dialog is open, the zoom). **Checks**: `tests/scripts/test_web_edge.py` and `web_edge_check.js` (the quick tier: the pixel over a bar, `clientFor` against Emscripten's own arithmetic, the locked cursor, the setting's block with a page of fakes, the listeners that must exist), and `tests/scripts/test_web_edge.sh` (opt-in: real headless Chrome with a REAL fullscreen and a REAL pointer lock, whose input gives the page real locked motion): on 1440 x 900 every side and corner of the bars scrolls the right way and the game's pointer is the clamped pixel, a walk from the picture into a bar goes on scrolling, back in the picture it stops, a click, a right click, a wheel and a ctrl + wheel over a bar reach the canvas as nothing, the windowed page is unchanged, fullscreen left with the pointer over a bar and the stage left leave the game's pointer gone, 2560 x 1080 and the classic 4:3 picture have their side bars, the page's own fullscreen does the same and asks for no lock; with the lock: the click on Fullscreen gives both, the cursor starts in the middle and follows the motion exactly (an independent copy of the rule), every edge and corner scrolls, a click opens the Options when the cursor is on its button, the release and the way out of fullscreen leave the cursor alone and unlock Esc, a click takes the lock again, a browser that refuses the lock still has the bars, the setting and its key, a slow mouse at device ratios 1 and 2.
