(The new checks fail on the previous page: 10 of the 20 bar checks.) **Not tested headless**: the Dock and the menu bar themselves (a real screen), that Esc is the game's key and holding it leaves fullscreen (the browser's own interface does that), the browser's own bubbles, Safari and Firefox (no Keyboard Lock API; Safari wants its gesture inside fullscreen: the click on the game is the second chance), a real mouse's acceleration and fractional movement on a Retina screen, a lost focus. **macOS desktop game**: SDL 2.32.10's window delegate answers the system's fullscreen question with FullScreen | HideDock | HideMenuBar when the window has `SDL_WINDOW_FULLSCREEN_DESKTOP` (read from the installed library's disassembly: `window:willUseFullScreenPresentationOptions:`), so `--fullscreen` and Alt+Enter already keep both away for good; a fullscreen Space that the green button or Cmd+Ctrl+F makes carries no SDL flag when the system asks and gets the system's own proposal (auto-hide: both come up at the edge), so `Application::update_mouse_grab` asks for `FullScreen | HideDock | HideMenuBar` itself while the window is in such a Space (`wants_hidden_dock_and_menu_bar`, `-[NSApplication setPresentationOptions:]` through the runtime, an exception caught) and gives the system's behaviour back when it leaves and at shutdown. Not tried on a real Space here (the Mac's screen was locked): the owner's Mac must.
* **The margin of a windowed page (`web/shell.html`, "The margin of a windowed page")**: the owner: "go off the screen with the mouse and it not screw up the scrolling" (agreed: keep scrolling while the mouse is just past the game's edge, about an inch, stop when it goes farther). The same cause as the bars: the browser says that the pointer LEFT the canvas the moment it crosses the edge, and the game takes that for a pointer that went away. A pointer that is outside the box but within `ANTS_EDGE_MARGIN` (96 CSS pixels, one CSS inch; a band around the box with square corners: `ANTS_PAGE.marginPixel`) is handed to the game as a motion at the nearest edge pixel (`edgePixel`), the canvas's leave towards the margin is not passed on (a capture listener on the window; a leave with no `relatedTarget` is the pointer leaving the browser window and is passed on), and the pointer is gone, as it always was, beyond the margin, out of the window (`mouseleave` of the document element), over a control of the page (`ANTS_PAGE.overControl`: `a, button, input, select, textarea, summary, label` and the roles radio and button: hovering the picture's and the mouse's selectors under the game or the links above it never scrolls the map) and when a scroll or a resize of the page moves the box away from a pointer that did not move. Clicks, the wheel and the context menu in the margin are the page's (SDL listens on the canvas; a button release that SDL sees without a press changes nothing in it); the browser's arrow shows there while the game draws its own cursor at the edge. Not for a frame of another page (`embed=1`: the box fills the frame), not for touch, not for a locked pointer, not in fullscreen. SDL 2.28.4 drops the FIRST leave of a page's life (see above), so before this change the first trip from the game onto a button of the page kept the map scrolling once; now the first motion of a mouse over the game is preceded by a leave of its own (`primeFocusOverTheGame`) and every leave is delivered. **Checks**: `tests/scripts/web_edge_check.js` (the quick tier: `marginPixel` on a table and on a sweep against an independent copy of the rule, `overControl`), `tests/scripts/test_web_edge.py` (the listeners and the constant), and `tests/scripts/test_web_edge.sh --only margin` (opt-in, headless Chrome in a 1800 x 1000 window: 1, 40, 90 and 96 px beyond every edge and 90 px beyond every corner scroll, 97 and 120 px do not, the selectors and links never scroll, a click, a right click, a wheel and a menu in the margin are the page's, leaving the window, scrolling the page under the pointer, a tap, the order of Chrome's and Safari's leave events, the first leave of a page's life).
* **Hardening from the security review (v0.0.84)**: the WebSocket output buffer grows with slack (a flood of pings cost quadratic time: 762,000 pings took 8.1 s, now 0.024 s); the control interface gives a new connection the slot of the oldest one that is waiting for its head with nothing unread (the door of the server does the same with connections that have not said Hello), so idle connections cannot lock either out. From the review of the release itself: all millisecond deadlines are wrap-safe (`clock.hpp`: the server's clock is its uptime and wraps after 49.7 days; a signed comparison against a deadline left at 0 stopped rooms from starting after 24.8 days); a seat that holds the match up for 20 s is dropped (`laggard_drop_ms`; protocol 8 replaced it: a server's room never waits, see "Who waits for whom") and a running room has a wall-clock limit (`max_run_seconds`, 2 h; demo rooms 30 min); a room with no keep time is still reported; the browser client says Hello when the socket opens (a hidden tab runs no frames).
* **Bot seats (v0.0.85, `docs/BOTS.md`)**: no wire change (`SlotState::Bot` is part of protocol 6). `HostLobby::add_bot` / `remove_bot` (a slot of state `Bot`: it counts as a player, its round trip is 0 so its thumb is good, the start never waits for it; Fog of War and a bot refuse each other in `set_fog`, `add_bot` and `start`; a person whose name starts with "Bot (" is renamed, so the slot state and the name are the only way to tell a bot), `HostSession::add_bot_seat` / `submit_bot` (the host acknowledges every turn for the bot seat, otherwise the sequencer would stall after `max_lag_turns` = 60 turns (3 s; 30 turns before protocol 8); the issuer is stamped by the sequencer; a bot seat never reports a hash and is never dropped for silence), `NetGame::add_bot` / `remove_bot` / `submit_bot`. The bot runs on the host's machine only; when the host leaves its bot seat leaves with it: the new host drops every seat of the roster that did not follow (the bot has no endpoint), the survivors stay identical (suite 2.20, AI5.5).
* **Not built yet**: a token check by the lobby (the bots of a server's room are built since protocol 11: see "Protocol 11"). Built since: the recording of every match as a replay file, the tool that plays it and the server's keeping of the matches of its rooms ([`REPLAYS.md`](REPLAYS.md)), the in-game host / join screens for `--join --room` (v0.0.97, "Joining and hosting from the desktop start menu", below) and the game's own clients for the way back after a lost connection (release B, "Reconnect", below).

## Protocol 7: the room's leader starts early (the first player of a server's room)

Request of the owner: "the first player to join the lobby should get a start button in case they want to start the game with less players". A server's room waits for every seat that it was made for; its **leader** can start it with the players who are there. Nothing else about the room or the match changes, and a LAN / direct game has no leader (its host starts, as ever).

* **Who leads** (`HostLobby`, `src/ants_net/lobby.cpp`): in a room of a dedicated server (the host holds no seat, `Config::host_seat = kNoSeat`) the guest who was welcomed first leads; when it leaves (it says Leave, is kicked, is thrown out for violations, or its connection dies) the earliest of the guests who are left leads. The order is the order of the Welcomes (`Guest::join_order`), **not** the number of the seat (a seat asked for with `--seat` or `?seat=` does not give the lead), a guest who comes later never takes the lead from one who is there, a guest who comes back is a new, last guest, and when everybody has left the next one to come leads. A bot seat is never a leader. The leader is chosen again (`elect_leader`) whenever somebody joins or leaves, **before** the room is broadcast, so the Room message that tells about the change already names the new leader. A host that holds a seat has none (`leader()` is `kNoLeader` = 255), and neither does a server's room made with `early_start` off. The lead is no authority beyond that one request, which only asks for what the room would do by itself with every seat taken; whoever knows the room's code and joins first leads it, so a lobby that wants to decide who may start makes its rooms with `early_start` off.
* **Wire** (`protocol.hpp`, `kProtocolVersion` 7 in v0.0.93; the history comment lists 2 .. 8, protocol 8 of v0.0.94 kept all of this and added `Lag` = 25, which is `MsgType::Last` now): `RoomMsg` has a last byte `leader` (a seat, or 255 = nobody; the same value goes to everybody, `you` is still per receiver) and a new message type `StartRequest = 24` (`MsgType::Last` in v0.0.93; client -> server; **no payload: exactly the type byte** in protocol 7 - 10; since protocol 11 the type and a fill level, two bytes). The decoder of the Room message is strict like the others: `leader` is 255 or a seat in 0 .. 3 **whose slot is a guest** (`SlotState::Client`; an empty seat, a bot and a host's own seat are refused), so the layout of protocol 6 (no such byte) and anything longer are no Room message; `decode` of a StartRequest accepted the single byte 24 and nothing else (protocol 11: the two bytes `24, level`, level 0 - 3, nothing else). Protocol 7 is **not compatible with 6**: the Hello still has its layout, so a client of protocol 6 gets `Reject VersionMismatch` from a LAN host and from a server alike. (Rewritten tests: N2.2 listed 24 as an unknown type, N4.1 changed the byte before the last one (`you`) and now also the last (the leader).)
* **The StartRequest rules** (`HostLobby::handle_guest_message`): a request from the leader, while the room is open (`Phase::Room`) and `can_start()` holds (at least `min_players` seats taken, one of them a person, a map chosen), becomes the event `LeaderStart` (with the leader's seat) for the owner of the lobby, which decides (the lobby never starts by itself). **Every other well-formed request is ignored and counted** (`ignored_start_requests()`): from a guest who is not the leader, from any guest of a host that holds a seat, in a room without early start, with too few players, or when the room is loading already (a leader who clicks START twice has its second request arrive after the Start). **That is no offence as long as one connection does not send more of them than a person could** (`kIgnoredStartRequestsAllowed` = 16 per connection: a double or triple click, a few tries on a slow link); **each one after the sixteenth is a violation** like any message that a guest may not send, so the 24th throws the sender out (v0.0.93: they used to be free, and a connection that sent them as fast as it could was never dropped; see "Flood control"). A request **with a payload is garbage** (since protocol 11: one that is not the type and a fill level 0 - 3): a violation at once (eight of them throw the sender out, and then the lead moves on). In the match the same rule holds for the session: a dedicated server's `HostSession` ignores a late StartRequest (the leader's click that crossed the Start on the wire) with the same allowance (`HostSession::ignored_start_requests()`, which the room's status adds to the lobby's count), a host that holds a seat counts it as a violation at once (nobody sends it one).
* **The room** (`ants_server`, `src/ants_server/room.cpp`): `RoomSpec::early_start` (default true; demo rooms have it on) is the control interface's `"early_start": true|false` in `POST /rooms` (a value of another type is a 400 naming the key). With it on the lobby is made with `min_players` = 2 and a leader; with it off `min_players` = the players expected, no leader is named and every request is ignored. When the leader's request arrives in a **Waiting** room whose lobby can start, the room starts at once with the seats that are taken (`Start` carries the roster mask, every client loads the map and reports, the match begins as for a full room; the map is checked against **that** roster, see below). A room whose seats are all taken still starts by itself, as before, with no request. `RoomStatus` (and the JSON of `GET /rooms`, `GET /rooms/<code>`) keeps `expected` as asked, shows `joined`, and has three new keys: `early_start`, `leader` (the seat while the room waits or loads, otherwise null) and `ignored_start_requests`. Rules where the early start meets the room's others: a request that arrives while the room is in the **pause after a cancelled start** (2 s, `Room::kRetryMs`) is lost, the leader presses START again; a request of a leader who has left in the same pass is void; seats that the map cannot be played by (a start marker outside the grid, `LevelData::validate(roster)`) are **not started early and do not fail the room** (a full room still fails with the reason, as it always did): the room goes on waiting.
* **The leader's screen** (`MapSelectScreen`, `Application`): the first player's setup screen is the original's **host screen** (animation `st_screen`: "Host Game Set-Up", Up / Down, START, Fog of War On / Off) in a **server room mode** (`RoomView::leader`; `is_guest()` is false, `leads_server_room()` is true): the map label shows the room's map (its name, the description from this machine's own copy of the file, "???" without it, exactly the guest's label), Up, Down and the wrap change nothing, the Fog buttons show the room's choice and change nothing (the buttons still show their hover and pressed pictures and click: they do nothing, as when a list has one map), **START (the button, Enter, the keypad's Enter, `S` and `s`, as on the host screen) sends `StartRequest`** instead of starting here (`MapSelectScreen::set_on_request_start`, `Application::net_request_start`, `NetGame::request_start`), `Q` / `X` / the Leave button leave as everywhere. The rows are the guest's (itself first, then the others in ascending order: nothing jumps when the lead moves). The prompt line is the host's own text (`kPressStart`, "Press START when all players' thumbs have appeared."); nobody else sees it (a guest keeps `kWaitingForHost`). **Too few players** (fewer than two in the room): the same as the host's START on a LAN room (`net_start_from_setup`): the can't-go cue (sound 63), no message box and no change of the screen, and nothing is sent (`NetGame::request_start` is false); the thumbs are not asked for (the host's START waits for them, a server's room does not). A request that the server cannot honour (a player left in the meantime, the pause above) gets no answer: nothing happens, the screen stays and the leader presses again. When the server's `Start` arrives (the answer to a request, or the room filled up) every screen is locked, a second click on START while the map loads sends nothing, and a cancelled start unlocks it. **A player who is not the leader keeps the guest's screen** (`nh_start`, only Leave works). **When the lead moves** (the leader left) the Room message that says so makes `NetGame::is_leader()` true on the new leader's machine and its screen turns into the host's at the next frame; the status line follows. No new text is shown on any original screen: every string is the original's (`src/ants_sim/game_strings.cpp`). **The quick help does not press the leader's START** (v0.0.93: START! of the quick help (529, 437) - (627, 464) lies under the setup screen's START (526, 439) - (624, 466)): a double click on START! has a second click that would press the new START, so on a machine that joined a room a left press that continues the click that closed the quick help is not given to the screen (`Application::swallow_closing_click`): one that SDL counts as part of the sequence (`clicks` above 1) and one that comes within SDL's double-click time of the closing click (`Application::kDoubleClickMs`, 500 ms), because SDL gives up its count when the pointer has moved more than a pixel between the clicks, which a hand does; a press after that time that SDL counts as a first click begins a new click: it is the screen's and ends the rule, as does creating the setup screen again; a held Enter or S that closed the quick help repeats into START, so **START of a room's screen ignores the auto-repeat of a key** (`MapSelectScreen::handle_key_down(key, repeat)`, `SDL_KeyboardEvent::repeat`), and the quick help's button is let go of when the window closes. **The original's own screens are not touched by the click rule**, because the original does not have this protection (verified in `Ants.exe`, `docs/GAME_REVERSE_ENGINEERING.md` 5.61): its window class (`FUN_01031989`, style 3: `CS_VREDRAW | CS_HREDRAW`) has no `CS_DBLCLKS`, so the second click of a double click arrives as an ordinary press: a local game and a LAN host act on the second click exactly as the original does. **The key repeat is no longer the original's on a LAN host's screen** (the review of the online rooms: an Enter that closed the chat input and was still held started a match nobody meant to start): its key translation (`FUN_01031bbd`) never reads the repeat bit of a key message, so the original's local screen and host screen start on a held Enter. **The local game's screen keeps that exactly** (no network, no chat input: a held Enter starts it, `test_hud_layout` "held keys", `test_app_integration` 9.14c, `test_network_app` N5.53); **the screens of a room do not: START needs a press on the leader's screen (v0.0.93) and on a LAN host's** (`MapSelectScreen::handle_key_down` ignores the repeat when `RoomView::networked`); Up and Down still scroll while held everywhere.
* **`--start-when N`** (N = 2 .. 4; 1 .. 4 since protocol 11, where 1 is a leader with `--fill-bots`): a client that leads a server's room presses START itself (the `S` key of the screen: the same path as a click) once N players are in the room, and again every second until the match starts. Off by default; anything but 1 .. 4 is no option. A headless client has nobody to click START, so `tests/scripts/test_ants_server.sh` uses it; the web front page's card gives it to the game of every match it starts (`&start=`, 1 + the Friend rows: "The front page", above).
* **Two notices of the games that a front page starts** (the one card of v0.8.0; the lobby's START hands every page its seat the same way): the game asks for the colour in `--seat`; when that seat is taken (an invitation that was opened twice, a colour that somebody else chose, a seat that the host moved the player out of) the room gives the first free seat (`N4.2b`) and the game's status line says so for five seconds, once: "**Green was taken: you play Red.**" (`NetGame::note_lobby_welcome`, N3.37; a game that comes back with its key sits where the key says and is told nothing). When the server cannot make the room that a Hello's create block asks for (the cap of `--demo-rooms` is full: every match of the front page, one against bots included, holds a place from its first Hello until 30 s after it ends; a running match that nobody is at gives it up after a minute, a waiting room after its ten minutes) it answers NoSuchRoom and the game says "**The server cannot make a room now: it is busy, or hosts no online matches. Try again in a few minutes.**" in place of "There is no such room on this server." (`NetGame::reject_text`, N3.17, N5.76b, RJ1.20); a Hello with no create block is told as before. Not done in the old addresses (the lobby's START waits for every person's game by name and has no such count): an invitation's seat is a colour, but `--start-when` counts heads, so a stranger or a second tab that takes a Friend's seat starts the match with the leader without the Friend (a seat mask in the address in place of the count is the later fix).
* **Not in scope (still open)**: short room codes, host migration for server rooms (the leader is the server's notion, the match has no host to move).
* **Tests**: `test_lockstep` N2.3b (the Room message with every leader value and every byte in the leader's place, the strictness, StartRequest as one byte and nothing else, 20,000 mutated Room messages), N2.1 / N2.2 / N2.3 (the new messages join the round trips, the type check and the 400,000-message fuzz), S2.5 (a late StartRequest in a match); `test_lobby` N4.10 (the leader: first welcomed, the earliest left when it goes by Leave / kick / cut / violations, a later guest never leads, none for a host with a seat, without early start or for a bot), N4.11 (only the leader and only when the room can start, everything else counted, a payload is garbage), N4.12 (the leader's side sends one byte, only from an open room that it leads); `test_server` S3.26 - S3.31 (a room for four started by its leader with two players and played to its end with the referee agreeing, a request that cannot be honoured, the leader who leaves, the pause after a cancelled start and a map that some seats cannot play, the control interface's `early_start` and the status keys, demo rooms, the same over real sockets); `test_hud_layout` (the leader's screen: art, buttons, rows, labels, START in every spelling, the lock); `test_network_app` N5.20 - N5.22 (the real application as the leader against the real room manager over TCP, as a player who is not the leader, the switch when the lead moves, `--start-when`); `tests/scripts/test_ants_server.sh` (a real server, a room for four, two headless clients, the first one starts it). **v0.0.93 adds**: `test_lockstep` N2.37 (the message budget), S2.6 and N2.38 (floods in a running match, 256 per update, honest rates), `test_lobby` N4.13 (floods in the room, 64 per update, the 16 free StartRequests), `test_tcp` N3.7 (the bounded inbox over a raw-socket flood), `test_server` S3.32 (a flood over real sockets next to a running match), `test_hud_layout` (a held START key on every kind of screen), `test_app_integration` 9.14c (the original's local screen is unchanged), `test_network_app` N5.23 - N5.26 (the quick help and the leader's START, by click and by key; what a click leaves behind does not outlive its screen; a LAN host is the original's), and the flood section of `tests/scripts/test_ants_server.sh`.

## Joining and hosting from the desktop start menu (`start_menu.hpp`, `application_menu.cpp`, v0.0.97)

A native game that is started without a mode shows a start menu (`CONTROLS.md`, "Start Menu"); its **Join with a code** and **Host an online match** panels use the game server's TCP game port (default `beta.playants.org:4001`, `--server HOST[:PORT]`, the settings key `server`) through `NetGame::join(address, port, name, 255, room, "")`: the same path as `--join HOST:PORT --room CODE --name NAME`. The protocol, the server and the lobby are unchanged.

* **Steps of an attempt** (`Application::MenuConnection`): the name of the server is looked up on a worker thread (`HostLookup`; `getaddrinfo` is the one blocking step of `TcpConnection::connect`; an address is answered at once; IPv4 is preferred when a name has both, the server listens on IPv4); then `NetGame::join` with the numeric address; then the first Room message (`Phase::Room`; a room that is full when the player joins sends Welcome, Room and Start together, so the menu accepts `Loading` too). The whole attempt has one time limit (20 s, `ApplicationConfig::menu_connect_timeout_ms`: a name that is never resolved, a connection that is never made); a server that accepts the connection and never sends its Welcome ends earlier, after the lobby's own 10 s (`ClientLobby::Config::welcome_timeout_ms`, `welcome_timed_out()`, `FailReason::NoAnswer`). **One rule for both: "The server <name> did not answer. Check the server's address and your connection."** A lookup whose worker thread cannot be started ("the system has no thread to spare") says so in its own line (`HostLookup::start_failed()`) instead of reporting an unknown name. Esc / Cancel abandons the lookup and closes the connection at once; nothing of an attempt stays when it fails (the tests pin the 20 s and the 10 s: `test_start_menu_app` A13.5).
* **Join**: the name (printable ASCII, 32; a name that starts with "Bot (" in any blanks and case is refused on the panel, the server renames such a player to "Player N") and the room code (as typed, the blanks cut: a code has none, the screens show it in groups of three; never case-changed: the room manager's map is case sensitive; the Join panel's Hello carries no create block, so it makes no room). On the Room message the quick help (the stored option) and the setup screen follow: the guest's, or the leader's host screen with START when the player was first.
* **Host**: the panel's map is Treasure until a choice is stored (`host_map` of the settings; a stored choice wins), like the page's. The code is made the way `web/lobby.html` makes it (six characters of `abcdefghjkmnpqrstuvwxyz23456789`, "Protocol 15" below) and the Hello carries the create block (the map, the player count, the Teams choice), so the server makes the demo room on the first Hello (`--demo-rooms`) and the player is its leader; the menu shows the code (large), the players in the room, Copy (the system clipboard) and Continue to the room. A room that fills while the code is shown starts by itself. A Hello that finds a room with that code already (about one in 890 million) makes the player a guest, not the leader: the menu leaves and says so. An abandoned demo room keeps its slot until it expires (the server's `demo_wait_ms`, ten minutes), so a player who hosts, leaves and hosts again on a server with few demo rooms may be told the server is busy. **A server whose `--demo-maps` does not hold the map that the player chose** does not refuse the code: it makes the room on its default map (`public_choice_of`), so the menu compares the map of the first Room message with the one it asked for and, when they differ, leaves the room and says "This server does not offer the <Map> map (its room is on <FILE>). Choose another map." (the menu offers the six maps of the original; test `A13.6`).
* **Failures** (each has its own line; `NetGame::fail_reason()` and `reject_reason()`, new, tell them apart; `ClientLobby::was_open()` tells whether the connection was ever open): `Unreachable` (the connection was never made: "Cannot reach <server>"), `Lost` (it was made and closed before the room: "was lost before you were in the room"), `NoAnswer` (it was made and the server said nothing for 10 s: "The server <name> did not answer"), `Rejected` with the server's `RejectReason` (`Full`, `VersionMismatch` ("This game is vX.Y.Z, but the server runs another version of the game"), `MatchRunning`, `Kicked`, `BadRequest`, `NoSuchRoom`: "There is no room with the code ... (capital letters matter)" for a join, "The server could not make a room now: it is busy, ..." for a host attempt), `Closed` (the room's connection ended once the player was in it).
* **The rest of a click** (`Application::swallow_menu_gesture`): a left press or release that changes the screen or the panel starts a rule for the click sequence, so that the second click of a double click, which lands on whatever lies under the same point of the next screen (Quit under the Host panel's Back, the Join panel's Back under Cancel, the setup screen's START under the quick help's), is not a press on it; see `CONTROLS.md` ("Start Menu"). A new panel preselects its first input and `Enter` / `Space` wait 300 ms (`StartMenu::kSettleMs`) after it appears; the menu's own clock decides (`update(dt)`), so the tests need no sleep for it.
* **After a match**: a network game that was reached from the menu goes back to it (`Application::return_to_start_menu`: the results screen's Leave, the setup screen's Leave, the quit dialog's Yes, `HostLeft`, a room that dies under the setup screen); the net object is destroyed and the next attempt makes a new one. The original's flow (Leave ends the program) stays for a game without the menu and for a single-player match.
* Tests: `tests/test_app/test_start_menu_app.cpp` (suite 3.12) runs all of it against a real `RoomManager` behind a real TCP listener and a raw server that answers each `RejectReason`, hangs up or stays silent; its `--real-server H:P`, `--real-fill H:P` and `--probe H:P` modes run the host / join / START scenario, the Host panel's "Empty seats at START" = Medium bots with one person alone (the server seats three bots; the tick rate is measured and must be 17 - 23 a second), and one join of a room that does not exist, against a server that is already running.

## Flood control (v0.0.93)

The review of the leader's START (v0.0.92 + the leader) found that **one connection could peg the server's only thread**: type byte 24 was a violation before the leader, so a client that sent it as fast as it could was dropped after eight; as a valid message that is ignored and counted it was free. Measured with a raw client (`u32` length + one byte, 20,000 frames per write) against a real `ants_server`: 91 - 95 percent of a core, the process grew by **1.1 GB in four seconds**, the sender was never dropped, and the same held for every other valid message that nothing counts (`Ping`, `Pong`, `TurnAck`, `Hash` of a turn that was never sealed, `Chat`: 70 - 96 percent of a core and 1.1 - **3.4 GB** in four seconds, chat lines relayed to everybody all the while). The cause was not the handling of the messages (a lobby takes 64 of a guest per update, a session 256) but the transport: **every `TcpConnection::poll()` read up to 256 KB and parsed every frame in it into an unbounded queue**, however few messages the game took (the WebSocket connection had an inbox bound since v0.0.81, the TCP connection did not). Three layers now:

* **The transport holds the sender back** (`tcp.hpp`: `kMaxInboxMessages` 4096, `kMaxInboxBytes` 1 MiB): at half of either bound nothing is read until the game has taken what is there, at the full bound not even what was read is parsed, and unparsed bytes (at most a whole message and one read) wait in the connection. The bytes stay in the kernel and TCP stops the sender; nothing is lost or reordered (a million messages, and a 64 KB message in the middle of them, arrive whole and in order: `test_tcp` N3.7); a hostile length prefix behind a flood still fails the connection when the parser gets to it.
* **At most N messages of a connection are taken per update** (the lobby 64, the session 256, as before; now that the transport is bounded the rest really waits for the next update instead of piling up).
* **A budget of messages per connection** (`flood.hpp`, `MessageBudget`): a token bucket of **1000 messages a second with a burst of 1000** (`kMessagesPerSecond`, `kMessageBurst`; `HostLobby::Config` and `HostSession::Config` carry them). A real client sends an acknowledgement for every frame that ran a turn (20 a second with turns of 50 ms, 80 while it catches up at 4x: never more than one per turn), a state hash for every 20th turn, a ping a second, its commands (the sequencer takes 64 a turn, 1280 a second with turns of 50 ms, and a person gives a few) and now and then a chat line. **Re-measured for turns of 50 ms (v0.0.94, `test_lockstep` N2.39)**, with the real sessions over a simulated link and the host noting when it takes each message: the busiest second of a client held **27** messages in steady play, **48** for a person who gives 20 orders a second, **89** for a window that catches up at 4x after a freeze of 10 s, and an uplink that was stuck for 25 s (the longest that a server's room tolerates: a player with no acknowledgement for 30 s is dropped) delivered **637** at once, within the burst; so the budget is some 10 to 40 times above what an honest client says in a second, and it stays as it was. A client at the sequencer's 1280 commands a second is no person and meets the budget first (an hour at 1000 a second is never refused, an hour at 1100 refuses exactly the surplus: `test_lockstep` N2.37; honest traffic of 900 a second for twenty seconds and a burst of 900 after a pause are not touched). **A message that finds the bucket empty is not handled and is a violation** (eight throw the sender out: `Reject BadRequest` in the room, a `Drop` in the match). A flood of 3000 pings, pongs, acknowledgements, hashes or chat lines at once is therefore answered (or relayed) a second's worth at the most and ends with the sender out, and the others never notice (`test_lobby` N4.13, `test_lockstep` S2.6 / N2.38); a sender at 1500 a second is out after about two seconds.
* **The ignored StartRequests have a count of their own** (`kIgnoredStartRequestsAllowed` = 16 per connection, see above): they are the messages that a person sends twice, so a flood of them is dropped after 24, not after a second's worth.

The server's main loop and the control interface did not change. Measured again with the same raw client against the same kind of server after the change: the sender is dropped after **0.01 s** (StartRequests: out at its 24th request, `ignored_start_requests` 24) or **about 0.4 s** (pings: a second's worth of pongs, 1000 - 1300), the process stays at **3 MB** and 0 - 1 percent of a core, the control interface answers in 2 ms, and a match in another room keeps its clock of 20 ticks a second. The same experiment runs as tests: `test_server` S3.32 (the real server loop and real sockets in one process, a victim match in another room: dropped within two seconds, no pass of the loop slow, the process idle again and not grown by 200 MB, the victim's clock kept) and `tests/scripts/test_ants_server.sh` (the real `ants_server` and a raw client written in Python).

## The browser as a client: hidden tabs, and sound (v0.0.96)

**The problem.** A browser stops `requestAnimationFrame` in a page that is not shown (another tab in front, a minimised window, a window that is completely covered) and slows the page's timers: to once a second, and after a while hidden to once a minute (measured in headless Chrome 154: the 100 ms interval of a page that another tab covered ran once a second for the first 58 s and then once a minute, with one call 13 s later and none in the rest of a 130 s measurement; Chrome documents this "intensive wake up throttling" for a page that has been hidden for five minutes and has been quiet, and spares a page that plays audio). The game's frame loop (`emscripten_set_main_loop`, driven by `requestAnimationFrame`) was the only thing that polled the connection and ran the lock-step runner, so a hidden seat stopped executing turns and stopped acknowledging them. What a dedicated server's room does with such a seat is the lag policy of v0.0.94 (above): the room keeps its pace of 20 ticks a second, the other players read "<name> is lagging (N s behind)" from the moment the seat is 3 s behind (`kLagNoticeMs`), and the server drops the seat after 30 s without an acknowledgement (`kLagDropIdleMs`, `include/ants_net/session.hpp`; in a two-player match the other player then won): a hidden tab was a lost seat after 30 s, and its player found "You were away too long and were dropped from the match." (Before that, v0.0.93's sequencer held the whole room as soon as the seat was 30 turns (3 s) behind and dropped it 20 s later; measured with that build, the built-in browser, Chromium 152, two seats, one of them hidden: the room's tick count stopped 4 s after the seat was hidden and the match ended about 20 s later. A LAN host, which has a seat of its own, still stops sealing while a peer is 3 s behind.)

**While the page is hidden the network drives the match.**
* *The wake-ups.* A browser slows timers, but the page's WebSocket events still arrive, and every message of the server runs the game's callback at once (they are events, not timers). `WasmWsCallbacks` (`src/ants_net/wasm_ws.cpp`: a message, an error, the close) calls `WasmWsConnection::set_on_wake` -> `NetGame::set_on_wake` -> `Application::background_pump`. The server sends a turn every `kTurnMs` (50 ms) in a match and answers the page's ping every second (in a room too), so a hidden page is woken at about that pace. The fallback for a server that is quiet is a timer of the page that calls the exported `ants_background_pump()`: a Worker's interval of 250 ms (`web/shell.html`, `window.antsBackgroundWorker`), which the browser does not slow in a hidden page (measured in headless Chrome 154: 18 to 40 messages in every 10 s of 150 s hidden, while the page's own `setInterval` ran once a second and then once a minute); where a Worker cannot be made, the page's own `setInterval` (`window.antsBackgroundTimer`), which a hidden page slows. A step may end the session (the host or the server left): the callbacks copy the function before they run it, because ending the session deletes the connection whose callback is running.
* *What a step does* (`Application::background_run`; the same `pump_network` that a frame calls): the network clock moves on, the room or the session runs (the connection is polled, `LockstepRunner::update` executes the ticks that are due under its own rules: one tick per turn, the adaptive jitter buffer of one to four turns, a standing backlog run down at up to 4x, at most eight ticks for every 100 ms of the step; the acknowledgements, the hashes and the pings go back), and the room and start events are handled (a Start that arrives while the page is hidden loads the map and reports, and the match begins). `post_tick` runs for every tick as always (the HUD's state, the news, the end of the match). A step draws nothing, runs no music chain and no SDL events. The lag machinery of v0.0.94 needs nothing of its own for a hidden page: a seat that acknowledges every turn as it arrives is never 3 s behind, so the room never sends a `Lag` notice about it (checked in a real hidden tab: none reached either page in 120 s), and "Catching up..." and "You are lagging" never come on in a page that is shown again.
* *No sound, no music.* A step makes no sound and starts no music, whatever it does. The sound effects of its ticks (the events are drained and dropped: not played, not saved up), the start voice of a match that begins, the buttons' cues and the can't-go cue (`Application::play_effect`) are not played. The music that the beginning of a match, its end or the loss of the server would start or close (`enter_match`, `check_match_over`, `return_to_map_select`) is not touched either: the last such wish is kept (`pending_music_`) and carried out once, by the next frame (a hidden page that still draws: a frame is what a page that is heard runs) or when the page is shown (`apply_pending_music`). The piece that plays goes on meanwhile (its sound comes from the audio callback, which the browser keeps running for a page that makes sound), and a hidden period in which nothing happened starts nothing. A page that is shown again also gets the original's own reaction to the window's deactivation (SDL's focus events are processed with its first frame: the music closes and a new random piece starts). Checked in a real hidden tab by logging `Module.FS.open`: a tab that was hidden from its start opened `INTRO.mp3` when it started (the setup screen's music: that is not a step) and no music file after that, while the match began and after the server was stopped under it (a guard of the start voice alone left `ANTS2A.mp3` opened at the start of a match and `INTRO.mp3` after the server stopped, with the page still hidden).
* *Who drives.* `Application::set_page_hidden` (the web build registers `visibilitychange` itself with `emscripten_set_visibilitychange_callback`, asks the browser at start and at every wake-up, so a missed event cannot leave a match without a driver; the tests call it) says whether the wake-ups MAY drive: a shown page is driven by its frames only, a local game is never driven by wake-ups (it stands still while the page is hidden, as the native game does when minimised: `SDL_WINDOWEVENT_MINIMIZED`). A hidden page whose frame loop keeps up with the turns by itself, with a frame at least once per turn (`kFrameAliveSeconds` = `kTurnMs`: an embedded browser pane, such as the built-in browser used for testing, reports every page hidden while the pane is off screen and still draws it at 120 frames a second; any embedder with background throttling off does the same), is driven by its frames, with picture and sound, as before, and the wake-ups stand down. A frame loop that is slower than the turns is slower than the turns and still acknowledges only once per frame: the wake-ups step between its frames, so that the acknowledgements follow the turns (the first version stood down for a quarter of a second after every frame and counted a frame for at most 0.1 s: on v0.0.93's runner a hidden page that still got four frames a second, 215 - 260 ms apart, ran 16 ticks a second and fell behind, measured in headless Chrome with a Worker as the frame clock; v0.0.94's runner runs a standing backlog down at up to 4x also in a long frame, so on this base that window would keep such a page level too, but with one acknowledgement per frame). A step never starts inside another (`advancing_`: a frame or a step inside a frame or a step is refused), and frames and wake-ups share one timer (`last_frame_time_`): the time between two of them is counted once.
* *Time.* A wake-up counts for the real time since the clocks were last advanced (a clock that went backwards counts for nothing). The network's clock, and with it the lock-step runner, gets at most one second of it (`kMaxWakeSeconds`): a page that was asleep must not be paid back more than a second of play at once (and v0.0.94's runner then runs what waited down at up to 4x within that step). The rest of a long gap is not lost for **the silence of the host**. The step hands it to the session after the connection has been read (`pump_network(dt, gap)` -> `NetGame::note_gap` -> `ClientSession::note_gap`), so that what waited in the link is heard first and is no silence, and a host that said nothing in that update has been silent for the gap too: the session's 10 s of silence (`host_silence_ms`) are 10 s of REAL time, however seldom the browser wakes a hidden page. A host is called silent only after it has been ASKED: a page that sleeps sends no pings, and a live host that holds its turns (a LAN host that waits for a lagging player, a match that is paused) is quiet but answers pings. So a wake-up that finds the host unasked since it last spoke counts the gap only up to `kAskGraceMs` (3 s) short of the limit; the ping that update sent is the question, its answer is a message, which wakes the page and is heard, and a host that does not answer has the gap held against it by the next wake-up (the ping is outstanding by then: `asked_`, the oldest question that nothing has answered). A live host, whether it streams turns, holds them or has data waiting while the page sleeps, is never taken for silent. The page's timer is a Worker's and runs four times a second, so a silent server is noticed after its ten seconds, as a shown page notices it; measured with a paused server (`docker pause`: the connection stays open and nothing comes) on the images of this release: a shown seat at +10.0 to +10.1 s, and seats that had been hidden for 22 s, for 75 s (the page's own timer already at once a minute) and for 150 s, at +10.0 to +10.3 s each (the connections polled every 0.1 s; polled once a second they read +10.0 s, +11.0 s and +10.0 s). Counting a second per wake-up, as the first version of this change did, would need ten wake-ups, which with Chrome's page timer at once a minute are ten minutes (measured with the first version and a paused server, on v0.0.93's 100 ms turns: a shown seat closed at +10 s and a hidden one at +606 s in the review, and at +10.0 s and +610.6 s when it was measured again, the seat hidden for 75 s before the pause: the silence rule does not depend on the length of a turn). Without the Worker (a browser that slows it, one that cannot make it) the same rule decides at the second wake-up after the ten seconds: with Chrome's page timer at once a minute, up to two minutes. When the page is shown again the last wake-up brings the clocks up to date, the frame timer restarts from that moment (the hours that the page was hidden are not one huge frame: the frame statistics and the FPS display never see them) and the frame loop has the match. (Measured: a seat that had been hidden for 57 s read "ping 3 ms" one second after it was shown, at 60 frames a second, and its screen showed neither "Catching up..." nor a lag line.)
* *The console.* One line per hidden period of a second or more in which the wake-ups stepped, at most one every ten seconds (`Application::hidden_period_line`; a tab that is flicked through says nothing): `The page was hidden for 45.1 s; the match advanced by 901 ticks and 1126 wake-ups stepped it in the background`, or, for a period spent in a room, `The page was hidden for 25.0 s; 51 wake-ups kept the connection going in the background (no match tick ran)`.

**What the browsers do** (what is said of Chromium was measured here, the rest is what the browsers document: Firefox and Safari have not been run): *Chrome and Edge*: `requestAnimationFrame` stops in a hidden or fully covered page; timers run once a second, and then once a minute (intensive wake-up throttling: chained timers only; a page that plays audio is exempt): in headless Chrome 154 the 100 ms interval of a page that another tab covered ran once a second for the first 58 s and then once a minute, with one call 13 s later and none in the rest of a 130 s measurement, and Chrome documents the minute for a page hidden for five minutes and quiet for 30 s. WebSocket events are delivered at once (measured, turns of 50 ms: a page hidden for 45 s was woken 1,126 times for 901 ticks: one wake-up per turn (900), one per pong (45) and four a second of the page's Worker timer (180); in a really hidden tab of headless Chrome 154, a tab opened behind another with its seat in a room for two, the room ran 2,404 ticks in 120.2 s, 20.00 ticks a second, the hidden seat's own hash reports gave 20.00 as well, all 24 hash reports that both games posted agreed, and no `Lag` notice reached either page (2,619 and 2,611 messages)). *Firefox*: the same (animation frames stop, timers are clamped to a second in a background tab, network events are delivered). *Safari* (macOS): the same, and a background tab may be slowed further or suspended to save energy. *Safari on iOS and every phone browser*: a page that leaves the foreground is suspended within seconds and its connections are closed by the system: the seat is dropped (its connection closes, or after 30 s without an acknowledgement) whatever the page does (a phone that locks its screen leaves the match). A computer that sleeps stops every page it has.

**A tab that the browser freezes** (Chrome's tab freezing, a page frozen through the DevTools protocol) stops completely: nothing in the page can prevent it. Its WebSocket does not survive it: the connection closes at the thaw with the close code 1006 (measured with a page frozen for 8 s, and a plain `WebSocket` in the same page does the same), the server sees the connection close and drops the seat at once, and the room's match goes on without it (in a two-player match it ended 0.3 s after the thaw, the finest the check could see). The server's own rule (it drops a seat 30 s after its last acknowledgement, `kLagDropIdleMs`; the others are told that it lags from the third second) only comes first for a tab that stays frozen longer than that: with a page frozen for 45 s the match ended 30.3 s after the freeze, before the thaw, and 29 `Lag` notices (one a second from the third second) had reached the other seat. Since v0.6.0 a room holds the seat of a frozen tab and the page comes back to it with its key ("Reconnect", below); the measurements of this paragraph are from before.

**Sound and the autoplay rules.** A browser lets a page make sound only after the person has done something on it, so the game's Web Audio context (SDL's, `Module.SDL2.audioContext`, opened when the game starts) begins suspended and the game's sound and music begin the moment it is resumed; the game itself does not wait for it, and there is no start overlay any more (the page used to hide the game behind a card that said "Click anywhere to play!" until a click). SDL's own Emscripten audio already resumes the context at the first `keydown`, `mousedown` and `touchstart` (`autoResumeAudioContext`: once each, on the document and the canvas); the old page's own tries did nothing (it looked for a global `SDL2`, which does not exist, and made a second context that played nothing). `web/shell.html` now keeps trying until the context really runs (no `once`: a press that grants nothing, such as the Esc key or a shortcut of the browser, or a press before the context exists, would use the attempt up), on `pointerdown`, `mousedown`, `click`, `touchend` and `keydown`, and takes its listeners off as soon as the context runs; a context that stops later is resumed by the next press (`statechange`, `visibilitychange`). What each browser needs: *Chrome and Edge*: a context created before any user activation starts suspended; `resume()` works after a user activation (`mousedown`, `pointerdown` of a mouse, `pointerup` / `touchend` of a finger or a pen, `keydown` of a key that is not Esc or a shortcut of the browser), and the activation is sticky: after the first press anywhere on the page a context created later starts running; a frame needs `allow="autoplay"` (the frames of `web/lobby.html` have it). *Firefox*: the same rule (a suspended context, `resume()` in an input event: click, keydown, touchend; the person may also allow audio for the site, then the context runs at once). *Safari* (macOS and iOS): the strictest: `resume()` must be called inside the handler of a gesture (`click`, `touchend`, `keydown`), it is not sticky, `touchstart` does not count on iOS, a context that a phone call or the lock screen interrupted (`interrupted`) needs a new press, and the ringer switch silences Web Audio on an iPhone. The built-in browser lets pages play at once (its context was `running` before any press), so the check there exercised the page's logic with a real context that was suspended by hand: a trusted click resumed it, a second suspension was resumed by the next click, with the context running the listeners were gone (no `resume()` call), and a press while no context existed raised nothing and the next press resumed it. The keyboard focus: the canvas takes it when the game is ready, and again at the first press inside the game.

**Known gap, and the checks that cover it.** No automated test reaches the code that exists only in the web build: `WasmWsCallbacks` and its wake-ups (`wasm_ws.cpp`), the wiring of `NetGame::set_on_wake` in `join_url` (`netgame.cpp`), `ants_background_pump` and the visibility registration (the `__EMSCRIPTEN__` blocks of `application.cpp`), and the logic of `web/shell.html` (the fallback timer, the sound unlock, the keyboard focus). A native test cannot receive a browser's events, so `test_network_app` plays the browser (`HiddenDuo` wakes the application once per turn, a virtual clock stands for the browser's time) and checks everything that is not the browser's. What covers the rest, by hand: bring up the stack (`docker-compose.stack.yml`), open a room for two players (`?join=/ws&room=k7m2xq&roommap=tiny&roomseats=2&seat=0`, then the same with `seat=1`) with seat 0 in one tab and seat 1 in another, put seat 1's tab in front so that seat 0's is hidden (`document.hidden` is true in its console), and read the room's `ticks` from the control interface (`GET /rooms`, the bearer secret from `docker exec ants-server cat /results/control-secret`) at the start and two minutes later: 20 ticks a second with both players still in it, and the hash reports that both games post on the broadcast channel `ants-sync` (every 100 ticks) equal. `tests/scripts/test_web_hidden.sh` does exactly this against a stack that is running, with a throwaway headless browser, and also checks that no `Lag` notice (message type 25) reaches either page and that the hidden page opens no music file (opt-in, not part of `./run_tests.sh`; it needs a Chromium-based browser and Python 3 and skips with a message without them).

**Tests.** `test_network_app` (suite 3.6, `N5.32` - `N5.44`, a native test cannot receive a browser's events, so `HiddenDuo` plays the browser: it seals the host's turns and wakes the application once per turn, and `VirtualClock` is the clock of its frames and wake-ups, so that no test sleeps for a window): the match runs 30 s with no frame and the host compares every hash, nothing is drawn, the machines end bit-identical, the console line says how far the match went (N5.32); one driver at a time, the wake-ups stand down while frames come at least once per turn (pinned a hair either side of one turn), a step inside a step or a frame inside a frame is refused (N5.33); a wake-up gives the network clock exactly one second at most (a gap of ten minutes, of five seconds, of one, of 0.4), never less than nothing, and the runner pays that second out under its own limits (at most the ticks that a second holds at its fastest speed), frames and wake-ups share one timer (N5.34); the hidden hours are not a frame (N5.35); no sound effects in a step, none of the buttons' either, sound in a frame (N5.36); the last wake-up when the page is shown (N5.37); a local game is not driven by wake-ups (N5.38); a page that is hidden before the match starts goes through the room, the start and the match on wake-ups alone, with no start voice (N5.39); no music starts, closes or changes in a hidden page, a match that begins hidden, one that ends hidden and a server that is lost hidden each leave their music for the page, once (N5.40); a seat whose hidden page gets a frame a second, four, 220 ms apart, fifty a second or none runs 20 ticks a second (N5.41); a host that was asked and does not answer is called silent by the wake-up after its ten seconds of real silence however long the page slept (2.9 s after the question no, 3.1 s yes; wake-ups a minute, ten minutes, 1.5 s apart), a host with data waiting and one that holds its turns but answers pings are never taken for silent with a wake-up a minute for three minutes, and the frames of a shown page hand over no gap (N5.42); a wake-up after the application has stopped does nothing, a clock that goes backwards counts for nothing (N5.43); the console's line says what happened in a room and not "0 ticks", and a tab that is flicked through says nothing (N5.44). Each rule was undone in turn on this tree (turns of 50 ms): 64 mutations of the code, one at a time, each against the whole suite (rebuilt after every change and again after every restore): 61 were killed by at least one test; the other three change nothing that can be seen from outside (the silence stamp of a session that is not in its normal mode is never read; `NetGame::note_gap` outside a match finds nothing to tell; and the stand-down of 0.05 s, which is the real one turn at 50 ms, so that "one turn -> 0.05 s" is no change at all). The stand-down of the first version (a quarter of a second) is caught by the pins of the wake-ups' steps (N5.33, N5.41), not by a slower tick rate: v0.0.94's runner keeps such a page level. Every test of the suite is independent of the turn length (`net::kTurnMs`, `LockstepRunner::kTickMs`): it passed on v0.0.93 with turns of 100 ms and two ticks and passes here with turns of 50 ms and one tick.

## Protocol 11: bots fill the empty seats at the leader's START, and chat in the waiting room

Request of the owner: "i think hosting a online game should be able to be started with bots to fill seats too and also have a pregame chat". This covers the messages, the rules, the server's bots, the API, **and the screens**: the desktop Host panel's choice, the web page's choice, the line that a player types in the waiting room, what the status line says and, on the 16:9 setup screen, the chat box and the leader's fill footer (below, "The screens and the application's hooks" and "The chat box of the 16:9 setup screen"). Nothing here changes the simulation, the lock-step rules or a golden hash: a bot is a virtual client (project rule 5), and a room without a bot runs no bot code. **Team chat** (a line for the team reaches only the sender and its ally) is a rule of the relaying host and changes no message: "Team chat reaches only the allies", below.

### The fill

* **The wire** (`protocol.hpp`, `kProtocolVersion` 11; the history line reads "11: bots fill empty seats at the leader's START, chat in the waiting room"): the leader's `StartRequest` (type 24) is **two bytes** now, the type and a **fill level** `u8` (`FillLevel`: 0 none, 1 easy, 2 medium, 3 hard). The decoder is exact: protocol 7's single byte, a level above 3 and any extra byte are no StartRequest (a violation like any garbage; eight throw the sender out). `HostLobby` hands the owner `Event::LeaderStart` with the level, `fill_level_name` / `parse_fill_level` / `fill_bot_name` ("Bot (Easy)", "Bot (Medium)", "Bot (Hard)": exactly `ants_ai`'s `bot_display_name` of the standard bot of that level, which a test pins) are in the protocol header. A Hello of protocol 10 gets `VersionMismatch` from a LAN host, a room's lobby and the server's door alike, **before** its layout is read (the layout of the Hello did not change, the messages did).
* **What the server does** (`Room::update`; only the **leader's** request, in a room with `early_start`, counts: a request from anybody else, from a room without a leader and from a loading room is ignored and counted as before): with a fill level the room first seats a **bot of that level in every seat that is still empty, up to the room's `players`** (the lowest empty seats first; a room for three with one person gets two bots, never more), named "Bot (Level)" (a person can never take such a name: the lobby renames it; a bot is always shown as a bot, rule 5), then starts as for any early start: **one person is enough** (the bots make up the rest). Everything else of the early start holds: a request that falls into the 2 s pause after a cancelled start is lost, seats the map cannot be played by do not fail the room.
* **Only the leader's START adds bots.** A room that fills up and starts by itself, a room whose waiting time ends and a room that is full when the request arrives add none; fill **none** is exactly the START of protocol 7 (one person alone does nothing, two start without bots).
* **Fog of War and bots never mix** (rule 5): with fog on the room refuses the fill, seats nothing and **tells the leader why** with a notice (a `Chat` message whose sender is 255 = the room itself, text `kNoticeFillFog` = "Bots cannot play with Fog of War.", to the leader only: `HostLobby::notify`); the match starts without bots if two people are there, otherwise the room goes on waiting. A map that the **filled** roster cannot play (a start marker outside the grid, `LevelData::validate`) is refused the same way (`kNoticeFillMap`), seats no bot and does not fail the room; the same map starts for the people alone.
* **A cancelled start takes the fill away** (a player who cannot load the map, a leaver, the load timeout): the room puts the bots it seated out again (`Room::unseat_fill`), so it is exactly what it was before the START: a person who comes next finds a seat, the status lists no bots, and a room "full" of bots does not start again by itself. The leader's new request seats them again.
* **The server runs the bots** as virtual clients, reusing the machinery of the bots of a LAN host (B1): `HostSession::add_bot_seat` for every bot slot before the match starts (the server acknowledges the turns for the seat, so the sequencer never waits for it), one `ai::BotController` per room over the **referee's own engine**, called from the runner's tick hook after every tick, and a sink per bot whose commands go through `HostSession::submit_bot` (the sequencer stamps the seat as the issuer, and the controller's filter keeps a bot to what a person can click: its budget in commands per second and its reaction delay are the level's `Profile`). A bot reads the world through the read-only `BotView` only; it sees nothing a person of its team could not (with fog it is never seated at all). **A bot seat is never absent, never votes and is never counted as connected** (reconnect, protocol 10): it has no connection to lose, `Attendance` knows the seats of persons only, so the vote is won by more than half of the **people** who are there; while the match is paused for a person who is away the bots' orders wait (`submit_bot` is refused), and they play on when it resumes (test S3.67: two people and two bots, one person cut, the vote has one voter, the survivor's vote drops the other, the bots play to the end and the referee agrees; the same with the person coming back with its key). The bots that sit in a match are the standard bot of the chosen level (v0.3.0: it gathers food, raids and fights back, and plays one of four styles that it draws from its own seat's generator or that the room's specification pins, `"bot": "hard:raider"`; the style is not on the wire and not in the seat's name; `docs/BOTS.md`, "The standard bot").
* **The bots wait for the match's "Get ready to play!" dialog too (v0.1.1, no protocol change; replaced in v0.2.0 by "Protocol 12" below)**: in v0.1.1 the bots did not look or order before tick 100 (`sim::kMatchStartHoldTicks`, the 100 ticks for which every machine's dialog took the person's clicks while the simulation ran behind it), and their bucket held one token until then. Since v0.2.0 the simulation itself waits for the dialog, so the bots need no hold of their own (no tick, no look); they open with one token (`docs/BOTS.md`, "Fairness in detail"). Their commands still travel as data.
* **The room's specification** (`POST /rooms`, `RoomSpec::bots`): `"bots": [{"seat": 2, "bot": "medium"}]`, `bot` being what `--bot` takes after the seat (`easy`, `medium`, `hard`, `idle`, `worker`, `KIND:LEVEL`, and a pinned style of the standard bot: `LEVEL:STYLE` or `standard:LEVEL:STYLE`, e.g. `hard:raider`; the styles are `aggressive`, `economic`, `raider` and `defensive`, a Hard bot plays the first and the third only); a mistake is a 400 that names the key (an entry that is not an object with a seat 0 - 3 and a bot, a bot that does not parse (a style on the worker or the idle bot, or one that the level may not play, names `style`), a seat twice, **no seat left for a person** (at least one of the room's players must be a person), **bots with Fog of War**). The bots sit in the room from the start, count towards `players` and are shown like the fill's; a person who asks for their seat gets the first free one; a room whose people have all come starts by itself, a leader's START with no fill starts the people and the bots that are there.
* **The status JSON** (`GET /rooms/<code>`, the list, the result file): `bots` is `[{"seat", "bot" (as the specification says it: "medium", "hard:raider" with a pinned style, "worker:easy", "idle:medium"), "kind", "level", "style" (the standard bot's only: the pinned style, "random" for one that draws its own at the start of the match; the key is absent for the worker and the idle bot), "name", "fill" (true: seated by the leader's START)}]` by seat, empty for a room with no bot; `players` and `joined` count the bots (a bot is a player; each entry of `players` has a `bot` flag, true for a bot's seat); a finished room keeps the list. `state_hash` (16 hex digits) is new for a finished room: the referee's state hash at `ticks`, which every player's game stands at the same tick (a lobby that keeps results can compare it; the tests do).
* **A LAN host's own START** gives the same option: `NetGame::fill_bots(level, &seats)` seats a bot in every empty seat of the room (a room on the local network has four seats) and the host's machine runs them as it runs `--bot` seats (`Application`: `--fill-bots`, below); with fog it seats none and the status line says why, a cancelled start takes them out again (`remove_bot`).

### Chat in the waiting room

* **The messages**: the match's `Chat` (type 9, `u8 sender, u8 team, str8 text`) unchanged. In the room (`Phase::Room`) and while the map loads (`Phase::Loading`) a guest's line is relayed by the room to **everybody, the sender included**, with the **seat of the connection** stamped (what the sender wrote there does not matter) and **no team** (teams are not formed before the match: the flag is cleared). Length and characters are the match's: at most 100 printable ASCII characters; a line that does not decode (too long, a control character, a flag other than 0 and 1, a cut message) is a **violation as in the match**; an empty line says nothing and is ignored (it costs a message of the budget). The room speaks to one guest with sender `kRoomSender` (255): the notices above.
* **The flood budget** is the connection's, as for every message (`flood.hpp`: 1000 messages a second, a burst of 1000), **and chat lines have a budget of their own** (`ChatBudget`, a remake protection: **the original has no limit on chat**): a **burst of 5 lines, then one a second**. A line beyond it is **dropped** (not relayed, not logged, no answer, no offence at first: a person who pastes six lines loses the sixth); a connection that goes on beyond it for long is flooding: 20 lines beyond the budget are tolerated and that allowance refills at one a second, so a talker who says **more than two lines a second** for about ten seconds (four a second: ten seconds; ten a second: three) has used it up, and **every line after that is a violation, eight throw the sender out** (two lines a second, half of them dropped, is tolerated for ever). 1500 lines at once are 5 relayed and the sender out within the same pass (`Reject BadRequest`) and nobody else notices; an honest talker, even one who says a line a second for two minutes, is never touched, and each connection has its own budget (a seat taken over with its key keeps the one it had). The same budget guards the **match's** chat (`HostSession`, a client's lines: the match had none before; the host's own player is no connection and has none). Review finding LOW-2: before it 900 lines a second for 10 s reached every player of the room, none of them was dropped, and the last 200 filled every match log and every `chat.txt` (`test_lobby` N4.18 / N4.20, `test_lockstep` N2.93 and S2.6, `test_server` S3.68).
* **Who hears what**: a guest who joins later hears only what is said after it joined (the room does not replay); a seat taken over with a key (reload in the waiting room) is the same guest and hears from then on. Lines said while the match loads are relayed too; after `Begin` the connection belongs to the session and the match's chat takes over unchanged.
* **The lines are kept** (`ChatLog`, the last 200 per lobby): `HostLobby::chat_log()` and `ClientLobby::chat_log()` hold everything that was said (a notice for this guest too), `take_chat()` hands each line out once. `NetGame` keeps them after the match has begun so that **the match's chat log can start with them: the application decides** (`NetGame::pregame_chat()`).

### Team chat reaches only the allies (no protocol change)

Request of the owner: "sending chat to all games isn't nessessary. Only allies should get ally messages and all should go to everyone". The match's `Chat` message always had a `team` flag, and the original's rule is the receiver's (`HUD::receive_chat_message`: a team line is shown only to its sender and to the player whose ally the sender is). Before this release the host relayed every line to every machine and the receivers dropped what was not theirs; now **the relaying host does not send it**.

* **The rule** (`HostSession::hears_chat(sender, team, seat)`, used by `relay_chat` for a client's line and for the host's own `chat_local`): a line for **all** goes to everybody (the sender included, as before: its screen ignores the copy); a line for the **team** goes to the **sender** and to the seat that is the sender's ally **now**, by the host's own table (`SimulationEngine::alliance_of(sender)`, the ally field of the team, the value that `WorldState::player_alliances` holds, but read without building a snapshot of the world for every line). It is the rule that a screen applies to what it receives, so nothing is sent that a screen would drop and nothing that it would show is withheld. A player with no ally who says a line for the team is heard by itself only.
* **At relay time**: the table is read for every line, so a break (`AllianceBreak`, a drop-out, a quit) takes effect **within one turn (50 ms)** (measured with real clients over TCP: team lines sent 0 - 50 ms after a Break still reached the ex-ally, from about 75 ms on none did; on a busy machine a line at 90 ms still got through once: the host's table changes when the host executes the turn that carries the break, one turn of 50 ms plus the time the host needs) and a new alliance (`AllianceAccept`) within one turn of its accept; the host's table is the one that has executed the turns that the host has sealed (the clients run a little behind it, by their jitter buffer).
* **Who relays**: the dedicated server's `HostSession` (the referee, no seat: its callback hears every line, which is not a screen; the server's rooms install none today, so **chat is not logged** anywhere) and the `HostSession` of a game on the local network (it holds a seat: it is a receiver like the guests, so its own callback hears a team line only as the sender or the sender's ally). A guest that becomes the host after a migration relays the same way. **The waiting room has no teams**: the lobby clears the flag and tells everybody (`HostLobby::relay_chat`, N4.19).
* **The receivers' own filter stays** (the original's rule, `HUD::receive_chat_message`): it is the second line of defence and the only one for a line that arrives from a host of an older version. No message changed, so `kProtocolVersion` is still 11.
* **Tests**: `test_lockstep` N2.90 (a dedicated server's host: the team line reaches the sender and its ally only, counted on the host's end of every link and read on the link of a raw client that has no session and reads every byte; a line for all reaches everybody; a player without an ally hears itself; the referee's callback hears all), N2.91 (a broken alliance stops delivery at once, a new one starts it), N2.92 (a LAN host is a receiver like the guests), `test_lobby` N4.19 (the waiting room), `test_server` S3.72 (a real room: a raw client of a non-ally gets no team line).

### What the screens use (the API)

```cpp
// ants_net/protocol.hpp
enum class FillLevel : uint8_t { None, Easy, Medium, Hard };  const char* fill_level_name(FillLevel); bool parse_fill_level(std::string_view, FillLevel&);
std::string fill_bot_name(FillLevel);                         // "Bot (Easy)" ...
std::string fill_level_title(FillLevel);                      // "Easy", "Medium", "Hard": "Empty seats will be Medium bots."
constexpr uint8_t kRoomSender = 255;  constexpr const char* kNoticeFillFog, kNoticeFillMap;
// ants_net/lobby.hpp
struct ChatLine { uint8_t seat; std::string name, text; bool notice() const; };   // seat 255 / notice(): the room itself
bool ClientLobby::request_start(FillLevel fill = FillLevel::None);   bool ClientLobby::chat(const std::string&);
const std::vector<ChatLine>& ClientLobby::chat_log() const;           std::vector<ChatLine> ClientLobby::take_chat();     // + Event::Type::Chat
bool HostLobby::chat(const std::string&);  bool HostLobby::notify(uint8_t seat, const std::string&);  bool HostLobby::can_start_filled() const;
// ants_net/netgame.hpp (what the application uses)
void NetGame::set_fill_bots(FillLevel);  FillLevel NetGame::fill_bots() const;   // the leader's request_start() carries it; with a level one person is enough
size_t NetGame::fill_bots(FillLevel, std::vector<uint8_t>* seats = nullptr);     // a LAN host's own START: seats the bots, 0 with fog
bool NetGame::chat(const std::string& text, bool team = false);                  // in the room and while loading (no team there); in the match as before
void NetGame::show_notice(std::string text);                                     // the status line, five seconds (the player's own line: "You: hello")
static std::string NetGame::start_prompt(FillLevel, bool fog);                   // what the status line says to somebody who can START with a fill level
std::vector<ChatLine> NetGame::take_pregame_chat();  const std::vector<ChatLine>& NetGame::pregame_chat() const;  // + Event::Type::Chat (seat = sender)
// the status line shows the latest line of somebody else's (or the room's notice) for five seconds: NetGame::status_text()
// ants_app/application.hpp
void Application::set_fill_bots(net::FillLevel);  net::FillLevel Application::fill_bots() const;   // the panel's choice, any time before START (--fill-bots sets the first value)
void Application::room_key_down(SDL_Keycode, uint16_t modifiers, bool repeat);   // a key on the setup screen: T opens the chat input, while it is open the keys are its
void Application::room_text_input(const std::string&);                          // SDL_TEXTINPUT
bool Application::room_mouse_down(int32_t x, int32_t y, uint8_t button);        // a left click on the status line opens the chat input (true: that was all it did)
const RoomChatInput& Application::room_chat() const;  bool Application::room_chat_available() const;
// ants_app/room_chat.hpp: RoomChatInput { open(from_key), close(), is_open(), on_key(key, mod) -> None / Send / Closed, on_text(text), take_line(), text(), caret(now_ms), end_of_frame(), kGuardMs }, RoomChatLog { take(lines, now_ms) }
bool Application::room_mouse_up(int32_t x, int32_t y, uint8_t button);          // the release of a press that the input took (true: nothing fires)
// ants_app/map_select.hpp: static std::vector<std::string> MapSelectScreen::status_lines(const IRenderer&, const RoomView&);   // what the prompt label draws: at most two lines of 14 px
// ants_net/session.hpp: bool HostSession::hears_chat(sender, team, seat) const;  ants_sim: uint8_t SimulationEngine::alliance_of(player) const;
```

### The screens and the application's hooks

* **`--fill-bots none|easy|medium|hard`** (any case; anything else, or no value, refuses to start; off by default) sets the level of this player's START when it can start a room: the **leader's** START in a server's room (the request carries it: START with one person is then no can't-go cue) and the **host's** START of a room on the local network. A game that is not a room ignores it. `--say TEXT` is a test hook for headless clients: it says the line once in the waiting room, as soon as two players are in it.
* **The desktop start menu's Host panel** has a fourth choice, **Empty seats at START: Leave empty / Easy bots / Medium bots / Hard bots** (`MenuId::HostFill`, remembered under the settings key `host_fill`: `none`, `easy`, `medium`, `hard`; anything else is none). Host sends it with the request (`MenuRequest::fill`) and the application calls `set_fill_bots` before the connection is made, so the leader's START carries it; the room's panel says what START will do ("Empty seats will be Medium bots." / "Empty seats stay empty."). A **Join** sets the fill to none: only the leader's START seats bots, and the choice is its host's. `--start-menu --fill-bots hard` starts the Host panel at Hard bots (nothing is written until the player changes it).
* **`web/lobby.html`**: the front page's earlier card (until v0.12.0) had the same choice for each seat (the radio buttons Easy, Medium and Hard: "Protocol 13", below), and the lobby that replaced it keeps the plan in the room itself (`Plan`, "Protocol 16"), where the server seats the bots at START; in the old addresses **every game link of the room carries the plan as `&fill=`** (the page cannot know who connects first, and only the leader's START uses it), also for a room that was made elsewhere (`?room=CODE&fill=hard`) and for a match made by the address (`?map=tiny&players=4&fill=medium`). Only those words are read, from the address (and, for the earlier card, from its memory), and `web/shell.html` turns exactly those into `--fill-bots` (a regular expression of the words; nothing else of the address reaches the command line).
* **What the status line says** (`NetGame::start_prompt`): to somebody who can START a room with a fill level (the host of a room on the local network, the leader of a server's room) "Press START: the empty seats get Medium bots." in place of the original's "Press START when all players' thumbs have appeared."; in a room with Fog of War "Fog of War is on, so START seats no bots."; a guest keeps its own line. A START with one person works for the leader and for a LAN host whenever a fill level is chosen.
* **Typing a line in the waiting room** (`RoomChatInput`, `Application::room_key_down`): on the setup screen of a room (waiting or loading), **T** or a **click on the status line** (the label at the bottom left, 36 x 447, 293 x 35; **on the 16:9 setup screen the click zone is the chat box's input box or its lines instead**, "The chat box of the 16:9 setup screen" below) opens a line to type; on the classic 640 x 480 page the status line shows it ("Say: hello_", printable ASCII, at most 100 characters) **as a typed line in the label's two lines of 14 px**: when it needs a third line the box scrolls by a line and the end of the line, with the caret, is what shows (a blinking caret is a blank while it is off: the wrapping never changes with the blink). **Everything on the prompt label fits the label** (`MapSelectScreen::status_lines`, review fix LOW-3: the label is 293 x 35, two lines of 14 px, as in the original, and a line of 100 wide letters made three lines that hung out of the black box): a text that needs more than two lines ends in "..." (the net also cuts a notice at `NetGame::kStatusNoticeChars` = 84 characters before that). **Enter sends it** (an empty line sends nothing) **and closes the input**, **Esc closes it** without sending. **The focus rule**: while the input is open every key goes to it and **S, Q, X, Enter and the arrows do nothing on the screen** (a line is never a START or a Leave), and **a click closes it** (review fix LOW-4: a touch screen has no Esc): any left press while the input is open, on the status line or anywhere else, closes it, sends nothing and is **not** a click on the screen (a click on START does not start the match through an open input; its release, and the release of a press that began before the input opened, fire nothing: `Application::room_mouse_up`); the input also closes when the match begins. **After it has closed (Enter, Esc or a click) START keys are held back for 400 ms** (`RoomChatInput::kGuardMs`, review fix MEDIUM-1: the reviewer started a match with T, a line, Enter, Enter, and with T, Enter, Enter, and with Esc, S): Enter, the keypad's Enter and S do nothing for 400 ms of the application's clock after the closing; a key after that is a new decision (Q, X, T and the arrows are not held back, nor is the mouse), and **START keys that are repeats of a held key never start anything on the screens of a room** (the leader's and a LAN host's: the original acts on a repeat, the remake does not there: a deliberate difference, `MapSelectScreen::handle_key_down` with `RoomView::networked`; **the local game's screen has no chat and is the original's: a held Enter still starts it**).
